The record in `maestro-claude-session-origins.json` holds several independent fields (origin, tab name, star, last context usage) written by independent paths, so every writer must MERGE its patch into the record rather than replace it. `claude:registerSessionOrigin` fires on every turn and used to replace the record with the bare origin string; that silently erased a third of all saved tab names, and those sessions disappeared from the Tab Switcher's "All Named" list and lost their titles in the Claude sessions browser, even though history still showed the names. `backfillClaudeSessionNames()` in the same file (run once by `stores/migrations/claude-session-names-backfill.ts`) restores lost names from the `sessionName` history stamps on each entry. Do NOT write `origins[projectPath][id] = ...` by hand at a new call site, and do NOT add a field to the record through anything but this helper.
- **Recording or reading the User Journey:** `sanitizeJourneyUrl()`, `formatJourneyLine()`, `normalizeJourneyEntry()`, `parseJourneyJsonl()`, `filterJourneyEntries()` and `describeJourneyEntry()` in `src/shared/userJourney.ts`; `appendJourneyEntry()` in `src/main/user-journey-manager.ts`; `recordJourney()` in `src/renderer/services/userJourney.ts`; `readJourneyFile()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/store/read-journey.ts` for a Node reader with no Electron. The journey is a per-agent JSONL at `<userData>/journey/<agentId>.jsonl` of what the USER did inside Maestro (files opened, saved, created, renamed, deleted; pages browsed). It is metadata only: never file contents or diffs. A recording site calls `recordJourney()` and nothing else: it stamps the time and the SSH remote and sends the entry over IPC without waiting, so recording can never block or break the action it describes. Browser URLs go through `sanitizeJourneyUrl()` before they cross IPC, because query strings, fragments, and embedded credentials often carry tokens; `normalizeJourneyEntry()` sanitizes `browser.*` targets again in the store, so a recorder that forgot cannot leak one. `appendJourneyEntry()` owns the file: O_APPEND writes through a per-agent write queue, the duplicate-navigate collapse, and the atomic prune to `JOURNEY_MAX_ENTRIES_PER_AGENT` / `JOURNEY_RETENTION_DAYS`. Do NOT write the file from anywhere else. The user can switch recording off (`userJourneyEnabled`, Settings > General > User Journey); the `journey:append` IPC handler is the one place that reads it, so a new recorder honors it for free. Readers (the main store, `maestro-cli journey`, the timeline) share `parseJourneyJsonl()` and `filterJourneyEntries()` and render each entry with `describeJourneyEntry()` (`formatJourneyLine()` adds the clock). Do NOT add a second line formatter or a second URL scrubber.
- **What happened around an agent, in time order (the timeline):** `readTimeline()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/store/read-timeline.ts`; `selectTimelineEvents()`, `formatTimelineLine()`, `timelineFollowUps()` and the per-source converters in `src/shared/timeline.ts`; `findHistoryEntries()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/store/read-history.ts` for `timeline show`. The timeline merges an agent's history entries, its User Journey, `git log --first-parent` in its working directory, and (only while the Encore Feature is on, and only when asked for) Computer History app switches into one list, oldest first. It is the tip of the iceberg: each line is a summary plus a drill-down handle (`[entry <id>]`, `[commit <sha>]`), the output ends with the commands that read more, and `timeline show <entry>` prints one turn in full. History is read from the END of the JSONL with `readRecentHistory()`, because busy agents carry several MB; do NOT read a whole history file to show recent turns. Git runs only for a local working directory (an SSH agent's is on the remote) and has a short timeout. Do NOT put the timeline (or any other per-turn value) in the system prompt: it changes every turn, so the provider's prompt cache would miss on every turn of every agent. The prompt points at `maestro-cli timeline`; add a source to the timeline rather than a new recent-activity section.
- **A `--since` / `--until` time the user typed (`30m`, `2h`, `1w`, ISO, epoch):** `parseTimeInput()`, `parseDurationInput()` in `src/shared/timeInput.ts`. Shared by `maestro-cli computer-history` and `maestro-cli timeline`. Durations reuse the Cue scheduler's `parseScheduleDuration()` and add weeks; an epoch is seconds or milliseconds by magnitude.
- **Serializing async work on one entity:** `createKeyedWriteQueue()` in `src/shared/keyedWriteQueue.ts`, re-exported from `src/main/utils/atomic-json-store.ts` so all seven main-process call sites keep their existing import. Each key (a session id, a `${sessionId}:${tabId}` pair) gets its own promise chain: callers mutating the same entity run strictly one at a time while different keys stay concurrent, and the chain continues past a rejection so one failed write cannot strand every later write for that key. Settled entries are deleted from the backing Map, which is what keeps it bounded in a process that runs for days. It lives in `shared/` rather than beside `atomicWriteJson` because BOTH processes need it and the renderer cannot import a module that pulls in `fs/promises` - the same hoist, for the same reason, as `assertSerializedJsonIsSafe` in `src/shared/jsonUtils.ts`.
- **Serializing a read-modify-write across PROCESSES:** `withDirectoryLock(lockDir, fn, { timeoutMs?, staleMs?, label? })` and `sleepSync(ms)` in `src/shared/directoryLock.ts`. `createKeyedWriteQueue()` orders callers inside ONE process; it does nothing when the desktop app and a `maestro-cli` process both read a file, change it and write it back, and an atomic rename only guarantees nobody reads half a file. The loser's update is still lost. This is the lock for that case: an atomic `mkdir` of a lock directory (atomic on POSIX and Windows, where an `O_EXCL` file is botched by some network filesystems), a bounded synchronous spin that sleeps through `Atomics.wait`, a `holder` file carrying a per-acquisition token, a steal of any lock older than `staleMs` (a crashed holder), and a release that deletes the directory ONLY while the token is still ours, so a holder whose lock was stolen cannot destroy the thief's. It was lifted out of `src/cli/services/agent-run-lock.ts` (`withStoreLock()`, which now only binds it to the agent-run store's directory and label) when the consult budget counter needed the same exclusion in the main process. Rules: `fn` is synchronous and must be short, because the app's callers hold the main thread while they wait, so pass a `timeoutMs` and `staleMs` sized to the work (the consult counter uses 2s and 10s; the defaults of 5s and 30s are the agent-run store's); keep `staleMs` well above the longest the guarded work can take, or a slow holder is robbed mid-write; the parent of `lockDir` must exist, and creating it is the caller's decision; and the lock is advisory, so it holds only when EVERY writer of the file goes through it with the same directory. A timeout throws `<label> lock timeout after <n>ms` without running `fn`. Do NOT write another `mkdir` or `wx` lock for a store, and do not reach for it to guard async work in one process.

  **A queue held in one process cannot order work performed in another.** A remote tab rename is therefore ordered twice, and the two queues answer different questions: the one in `src/main/web-server/callbacks/tabCallbacks.ts` orders the PROTOCOL - it is what puts `rename_tab_result` frames back on the wire in request order - while the one in `src/renderer/hooks/remote/useRemoteIntegration.ts` orders the WORK, which is where a rename actually happens (provider metadata write, history relabel, store patch). The server's queue deliberately stops waiting after `RENAME_CONFIRMATION_RELEASE_MS`, so an unresponsive renderer cannot wedge a tab; at that moment it frees the slot and dispatches the next rename while the abandoned one may still be running in the renderer. Those two then overlap and the older one can finish last, overwriting the newer name in both the provider metadata and the store - the exact lost update the queue was added to prevent, reopened past the release window. Both queues key on `${sessionId}:${tabId}`, so ordering is per tab and a rename never becomes an app-wide lock.

  Three rules for a renderer-side queue. It must be at MODULE scope, never a ref: the listener is registered from an effect that can re-run, and a queue rebuilt on re-registration forgets the work still in flight (`group-chat-storage.ts` holds its own at module scope for the same reason). Its slot must be handed on after a bounded budget as well as on settle, because `ipcRenderer.invoke` never times out - a main-process handler that never returns leaves its promise pending for the life of the window, and every later rename for that tab would sit behind it unattempted. And handing the slot on early is not cancellation: an in-flight IPC cannot be recalled, so the writer re-reads the newest REQUESTED name when its own write lands and writes once more if it was superseded, which is what turns a stale write that lands late into a self-correcting one instead of the final word. Do NOT hand-roll another `Map<string, Promise<void>>` chain.

- **Capping stored tool output:** `MAX_PERSISTED_TOOL_OUTPUT_CHARS`, `compactToolOutput()` and `compactSessionToolOutputs()` in `src/shared/toolOutput.ts`. A tool result is transcript evidence, but it is also copied into the renderer store, `maestro-sessions.json`, and the entire `sessions:getAll` web bootstrap. One Antigravity command stored a 67MB one-line result, which grew the bootstrap frame to 126MB and made iOS appear disconnected while it parsed the same Maestro instance. Cap at parser ingestion to stop new growth, at renderer persistence for every provider, and on main-process load to heal old files before any browser receives them. Short values retain their original type; oversized objects become a JSON preview because preserving a large object defeats the cap. Do not add another provider-specific limit or remove the load migration: historical sessions survive parser fixes until a storage boundary rewrites them.
- **Merging browser-deferred conversations:** `projectWebSession()`, `readDeferredContent()`, and `mergeDeferredSessionContent()` in `src/main/stores/deferred-session-content.ts` (pure; the persistence handlers own the store), over `mergeDeferredItems()` and `MAX_PERSISTED_SESSION_LOGS` in `src/shared/deferredSessionContent.ts`. `sessions:getBootstrap` omits AI tab logs, legacy session logs, and command history so a phone does not parse the whole sessions file at startup. The `deferredContent` marker follows that browser record through the renderer store. On either write path (`sessions:setMany` and `sessions:setAll`), main merges untouched fields from its full stored record, then removes the marker before saving. A selected tab and legacy shell output load through `sessions:getDeferredContent`; merging by log id keeps entries that arrived while the read was in flight. A stale browser tab already closed by another client is dropped without blocking the rest of the save. Do not persist a projected record without this merge, or a routine browser save erases every unloaded conversation. Every Retry control (failed agent read, unloaded conversation) raises `requestWebBridgeReconcile()` from `src/renderer/services/webBridgeReconcile.ts`; `useSessionRestoration` is the one listener.
- **Turning Live mode (the web interface) or Remote Control (its tunnel) on or off:** `getLiveModeController()` / `createLiveModeController()` in `src/main/web-server/live-mode.ts`, wire types (`LiveModeStatus`, `live_command`) in `src/shared/liveMode.ts`. The Left Bar LIVE pill (`live:set`), auto-start, the port-change restart (`live:startServer` / `live:stopServer`), `live:disableAll`, and `maestro-cli live on|off` (the CLI-only `live_command` WS message) all switch through this ONE controller. Before it, the switch logic lived half in `useLiveMode` (tunnel stop, then `disableAll`) and half in the IPC handlers, Live state was per-window React state, and two concurrent `live:startServer` calls (auto-start with a restored secondary window) could each create a server, orphaning one on 0.0.0.0 with a token nothing showed or stopped. Invariants: Live means `isActive() && isLanAccessible()`, never a separate flag; every switch runs single-flight on one queue key; `set()` is idempotent, so `on` while on never rotates the token (a raw `startServer()` does, which is what the port-change restart wants); `set(false)` stops the tunnel first; every finished switch bumps `revision`, records `lastError`, and broadcasts `live:stateChanged` to every window, which `useLiveMode` follows. Traps: a switch replaces the server a CLI socket is on, so the `live_command` handler replies FIRST (`ctx.send(..., onSent)`) and the CLI reconnects and polls `revision`; the `url` is reported only while Live is on. Remote Control (the Cloudflare tunnel) rides the same controller and queue: `startRemoteControl()` / `stopRemoteControl()` back the panel's `tunnel:*` IPC and `maestro-cli live tunnel on|off` (`remote-on` / `remote-off` actions, answered AFTER the switch since the server is untouched). `startRemoteControl()` REFUSES while Live is off, because the only server then is the loopback CLI server and tunneling it would publish the CLI's control channel to the internet. The tunnel manager is token-agnostic, so `remoteControlUrl` gets the server's token path appended here, never at a caller.
- **Asking a renderer window a question from main (request, reply, timeout):** `requestFromRenderer(win, channel, { fallback, parse, timeoutMs, args })` in `src/main/web-server/callbacks/remoteRequest.ts`. It mints a per-call response channel, sends it as the last argument on `channel`, and resolves with the window's reply (mapped by `parse`) or with `fallback` when the window stays silent for `timeoutMs` (default 3 s) or the send throws. It never rejects, it arms its timer before it sends (a window that answers synchronously would otherwise hit `clearTimeout` on an uninitialized binding), it drops replies from any other `webContents`, and it removes its listener on every exit. Do NOT hand-roll another `ipcMain.once(responseChannel, ...)` + `setTimeout`: over 40 copies exist in `src/main/web-server/callbacks/` and `src/main/plugins/run-ui-command.ts`, and the older ones skip the sender check or leak the listener on timeout. The older `createRemoteRequest(getMainWindow)` in the same file asks the main window only; prefer `requestFromRenderer` for new code. To tell "no reply" from a real answer, pass a sentinel `fallback` (a `Symbol`) and classify it after, as `requestAutoRunSnapshots()` in `src/main/debug-package/autorun-snapshots.ts` does; that function is also the pattern for asking every app window at once (`windowRegistry.getAppWindows()`, never `BrowserWindow.getAllWindows()`, whose feature windows never answer and each cost a full timeout).
- **HTTP request from the main process:** `fetchWithTimeout(url, options?, timeoutMs?)` in `src/main/utils/fetchWithTimeout.ts`, with `isFetchTimeoutError()` and `DEFAULT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS`. A bare `fetch()` has no timeout, so a stalled socket hangs the caller forever: in the main process that is an IPC handler that never settles and a renderer spinner that never stops. Do NOT hand-roll `new AbortController()` + `setTimeout`, and do NOT add another local `fetchWithTimeout` - there used to be three functions with that exact name and three different signatures. It composes a caller-supplied `signal` rather than overwriting it, so passing your own cancellation still works. Callers needing extra behaviour wrap it locally (see `fetchBmadResource()` in `src/main/bmad-manager.ts`).
- **Choosing a port for a local server:** `pickFreePort(host)`, `probePort(port, host)` and `isTcpPort()` in `src/main/utils/tcp-port.ts`. A server Maestro runs on this machine (the token proxy, the model server) chooses its port ONCE with `pickFreePort` and persists it, because something the user configured names that port: a port that moved between launches would leave the agent gateway, or a harness set up by hand, pointing at nothing or at whatever took the old one. At each start `probePort` binds the port for a moment, so a port another program holds fails by name (`EADDRINUSE`) before anything is spawned. The answer can go stale between the probe and the real bind, so the server's own bind failure still has to be handled. Never move a persisted port to dodge a conflict; refuse the start and name the address. The token proxy carried a private copy of `pickFreePort` until the model server needed the same two operations.
- **A size-capped log file for a supervised process:** `openCappedLogFile(filePath, { maxBytes?, onError? })` in `src/main/utils/capped-log-file.ts`, with `DEFAULT_CAPPED_LOG_BYTES` (5 MiB) and `CAPPED_LOG_PREVIOUS_SUFFIX`. For the stdout and stderr of a child process Maestro supervises (the model server's `userData/logs/model-server.log`). A write that would pass the cap first moves the file to `<name>.1`, replacing the one before, so a chatty process takes at most twice the cap; a single chunk larger than the cap keeps its newest part. It appends across opens, so a restarted process continues the same log. Writes are synchronous, which keeps the rotation from racing a stream that is still flushing. A failed open, write or rotation is reported once through `onError` and every later write is dropped: losing a log must never take the process, or its supervisor, down with it. The app's own debug log (`src/main/utils/logger.ts`) rotates by date and is a different thing.
- **Running and reaching the Maestro model server (`llama-server`):** `startModelServer()`, `stopModelServer()`, `getModelServerStatus()` and `ensureModelServerFor(modelId)` in `src/main/local-models/server/supervisor.ts`, with `getModelServerReport()` and `installModelServerEngine()` in `server/service.ts` (what the card, IPC, WS and `maestro-cli models server` call). The supervisor owns the process: a persisted port (`probePort` before spawn, never moved), a sealed key handed over a 0600 `--api-key-file` that exists only while the server starts, router mode with `--models-max 1`, a `/health` watch, and a crash restart with backoff (3 tries, then `failed`). A consumer never spawns `llama-server` or `fetch`es its port: it names a task and the router reaches the server through the built-in `maestro-server` endpoint (`server/server-endpoint.ts`), so a model the server already holds is never loaded a second time in-process. `ensureModelServerFor` returns `{ ok: false, code, error }` and never falls back to another source. Detail: the "Model server" section of [docs/architecture/local-models.md](../architecture/local-models.md).
- **Which `llama-server` build a platform gets:** `selectServerBuild(platform, arch, choice)`, `availableServerBuildChoices()`, `serverBuildDownloadBytes()` and `LLAMA_SERVER_RELEASE` in `src/shared/local-models/server-engine.ts`. The one table of release assets, each pinned by sha256 (taken from the release API once, never fetched at run time). It is NOT part of `NATIVE_RUNTIMES`: that registry models npm packages loaded in-process, and `llama-server` is a supervised executable with several builds per platform. A choice with no build (`cpu` on Apple silicon) returns `null` and the installer throws `ServerBuildUnavailableError` by name; never substitute another build, and never offer CUDA or ROCm as a default (large, and they need a matching driver).
- **What the model server keeps between launches (port, bind, key):** `readModelServerConfig(dataDir)`, `ensureModelServerPort(dataDir)`, `writeModelServerSettings()`, `ensureModelServerKey(dataDir, osSeal)` and `rotateModelServerKey()` in `src/main/local-models/server/config-store.ts`, over the file `model-server.json` (`MODEL_SERVER_STATE_FILE`). It holds the port, the bind mode, `maxModels` and the API key. **The port is chosen once and kept**: what points at the server names it (a harness set up by hand, the endpoint Maestro registers), so a port that moved between launches would leave all of that pointing at nothing. When the port is taken at start, the start fails by name; never move it. The key is generated with the `sk-maestro-` prefix and sealed through the `SealProvider` the caller hands in (`safeStorageSeal()` in the app); where the OS offers no encryption it is stored obscured and the log says so, and a stored key that cannot be unsealed throws `key-unavailable` rather than minting a second one. The file is a `createPrivateStateFile()` (`src/main/utils/sealed-state-file.ts`), so its read-modify-write is serialized and a start and a settings edit never interleave. Do NOT write `model-server.json` from anywhere else.
- **What the model server is started with (presets file, command line, environment):** `collectServerModels()`, `renderModelServerPresets(models)`, `buildModelServerArgs(options)`, `modelServerEnvironment(base, cacheDir)`, `serverContextSize(entry)` and `isServerModel(entry)` in `src/main/local-models/server/presets.ts`. The server runs in `llama-server` router mode, and the presets file (`--models-preset`) is regenerated from the installed text models at every start, one section per catalog id. Everything but `collectServerModels()` is pure. Three traps are handled here: the presets dialect has no quoting (a `;` or `#` in a model path starts a comment, so such a path is refused by name instead of opened truncated); a model with no `defaultContext` runs at the agent floor (`MODEL_SERVER_AGENT_MIN_CONTEXT`), because a smaller context fails a harness's first prompt; and the environment is Maestro's with every `LLAMA_*` variable removed, because `llama-server` reads each option from `LLAMA_ARG_*` when the command line is silent, so a variable left in the user's shell would reconfigure the server (take it out of router mode, add a key nobody chose, serve someone else's folder). Never spawn `llama-server` with an argv assembled anywhere else.
- **Where the model server's engine is installed and whether it is really there:** `serverEngineDir()`, `serverEngineStagingDir()`, `readServerEngineManifest()`, `writeServerEngineManifest()`, `installedServerBinary()`, `isServerEngineInstalled()`, `isServerEngineStale()` and `removeServerEngine()` in `src/main/local-models/server/engine-store.ts`. The engine lives in `userData/runtimes/llama-server/` beside the npm runtimes, and NOT beside the models: an engine and a model have different lifetimes, and "reclaim my models' disk" must not uninstall the engine. **It counts as installed only when its manifest exists AND the binary the manifest names is on disk**, the same invariant as `runtime-store.ts`; test that through `installedServerBinary()`, never `fs.existsSync(manifest)`. `isServerEngineStale()` is true when the release or a pinned asset hash differs from what `selectServerBuild()` would pick today, or the platform no longer has that build; the Download button then fetches the pin. The installer extracts into the `.staging` sibling and renames, so a half-extracted engine is never one that counts as installed.
- **What the installed engine computes on (GPU or CPU):** `parseListDevices(stdout)`, `classifyAcceleratorProbe(input)`, `acceleratorLabel(device)`, `serverAcceleratorProblemMessage(report)` and `LIST_DEVICES_FLAG` in `src/shared/local-models/server-accelerator.ts` (pure, so the Windows and Linux outputs are unit tested with captured text); `checkServerAccelerator(binary, kind, options?)` and `recheckServerAccelerator()` in `src/main/local-models/server/accelerator.ts` (what the installer and the card's re-check call). A build's name is a promise, not a fact: the Vulkan build on a machine with no Vulkan loader, or whose only device is the llvmpipe software renderer, starts and then runs every model on the CPU. So after an install the engine is asked (`llama-server --list-devices`, spawned with no shell, so a path with spaces needs no quoting) and the verdict (`gpu | cpu | failed` plus a problem code such as `vulkan-loader-missing`) is stored in the engine manifest. The check never throws and never undoes an install; a build that cannot reach a GPU is REPORTED and the CPU build is OFFERED where one exists. Nothing here swaps a build.
- **The model server's shared vocabulary (bind modes, status, report, endpoint reference):** `ModelServerBind` (`MODEL_SERVER_BINDS`, `DEFAULT_MODEL_SERVER_BIND`, `MODEL_SERVER_BIND_HOSTS`, `modelServerBindLabel()`), `ModelServerStatus`, `ModelServerReport`, `ModelServerErrorCode`, `ModelServerEnsureResult`, `modelServerBaseUrl(port)`, `MODEL_SERVER_ENDPOINT_ID` / `modelServerEndpoint(baseUrl)` / `isModelServerEndpointRef()` and the limits (`DEFAULT_MODEL_SERVER_MAX_MODELS`, `MAX_MODEL_SERVER_MAX_MODELS`, `MODEL_SERVER_AGENT_MIN_CONTEXT`) in `src/shared/local-models/model-server.ts`. Data and pure functions only, so the supervisor, the Models card and `maestro-cli models server` cannot disagree about a bind mode, a state or an error code. The default bind is `local` (127.0.0.1); `network` (0.0.0.0) is an explicit choice. The server is reached through the built-in `maestro-server` endpoint, so a consumer holds an endpoint reference, never a host and port.
- **Whether a model fits this computer (Fits / Tight / Too large):** `modelFit(entry, profile, context?)`, `modelMemoryNeed()`, `estimateKvBytesPer1kTokens()`, `estimateKvLayout()`, `largestVramGpu()`, `describeHardwareProfile(profile)`, `hardwareProfileStat(profile)`, `hardwareFitCaveat(profile)` and `MODEL_FIT_LABELS` in `src/shared/local-models/model-fit.ts`, fed by `resolveHardwareProfile()` in `src/main/local-models/hardware.ts`. One rule for every surface that labels a model (the Library row, `maestro-cli models list --fit`, a setup offer): weights + the KV cache at the context the server runs it at (`defaultContext`, from the entry's `memoryHint`; the size table only for a hand-added model with no attention shape) + system headroom. Unified memory (Apple Silicon, and an x64 build under Rosetta) fits within 75% of RAM after 2 GB headroom and is tight to 90%; a discrete GPU fits within its VRAM, a MoE that spills is tight within VRAM + RAM less 4 GB, a dense one that spills is too large; CPU only is never better than tight and refuses 10B active parameters and up. Every verdict carries a one-sentence reason with the numbers. The profile never runs a binary: GPU memory comes from Electron's DxDiag tree when present (rare) and otherwise from the device list the Phase 04 accelerator check stored in the engine manifest, so before an engine is installed a PC with a GPU is judged by the CPU-only rule. Do NOT compare `entry.bytes` to `os.totalmem()` anywhere else: the KV cache is often as large as the weights (Qwen3 1.7B needs 4.4 GB of KV at 40,960 tokens beside a 1.1 GB file). A surface that shows fit labels also shows `describeHardwareProfile()` (what was judged against) and `hardwareFitCaveat()` (the sentence for a GPU with no memory reading), and labels nothing until the profile is read (renderer: `useLocalModelsHardware()` in `src/renderer/hooks/localModels/useLocalModelsHardware.ts`, which re-reads when an engine install finishes). A model with `memoryHint.estimated` says "about" for its context share. `hardwareProfileStat()` returns the same memory facts as a headline value and caption for the Models Overview stat tile.
- **Grouping and badging models in the Library:** `groupLocalModelsForLibrary(items, entryOf, section?)`, `localModelLibraryGroup(entry)`, `localModelLibrarySection(entry)`, `localModelRoleBadges(entry)`, `recommendedLocalModelsForHardware(entries, profile)`, `LOCAL_MODEL_LIBRARY_SECTIONS` / `LOCAL_MODEL_LIBRARY_SECTION_IDS`, `LOCAL_MODEL_LIBRARY_GROUPS`, `LOCAL_MODEL_ROLE_LABELS`, `CLOUD_ONLY_MODELS` / `CLOUD_ONLY_NOTE` in `src/shared/local-models/library.ts`. The one grouping behind Settings > Models > Library and `maestro-cli models list`. The Library is SECTIONS (the tabs of its strip: Engines, Agent Models, Housekeeping, Speech, Search & Safety, Custom) holding GROUPS (one card each); every group is in exactly one section. A text model is listed ONCE: in `agent` if it passed the agent qualification, else in `housekeeping`; judge and vision are badges only (they were one- and two-model groups, folded in to cut the Library to six tabs). The agent badge reads "Agent-ready" and names the harnesses from `agentHarnesses` through `getAgentDisplayName()`. A model the user added (`userAdded`) has its own group, in Custom; every other kind is a group by kind. Do NOT list a model under each of its roles: a row carries live state (a download, a removal warning) and two copies of it disagree. Do NOT group text models by kind again (`groupByLocalModelKind()` was removed for this). `recommendedLocalModelsForHardware()` picks curated models that comfortably fit the shared hardware profile; no hardware, pending entries and user-added models receive no recommendation. It is a memory-fit hint, never a speed benchmark. The Library hides a `too-large` model behind a toggle, except one with bytes on disk or a download in flight; the CLI hides nothing.
- **The model server on a renderer surface:** `useModelServer()` and `MODEL_SERVER_POLL_MS` in `src/renderer/hooks/localModels/useModelServer.ts`. Returns `report` (the `ModelServerReport`, null until the first read), `progress` (the engine install running now), `busy` (`start | stop | set | rotate | install | null`), `error`, `refresh()` and the card's actions `start()`, `stop()`, `setBind()`, `setMaxModels()`, `rotateKey()`, `readKey()` (for Copy) and `installEngine(choice)`. **Mounting reads the report and nothing else**: it never starts the server and never downloads the engine, which is the "nothing starts itself" rule. The report is re-read when main says the state changed, after every action, and every `MODEL_SERVER_POLL_MS` while the server runs, because a model loads on demand with no state change. Do NOT poll `window.maestro.localModels` for the server from a second component.
- **Naming where a model choice lives in Settings (in a message or error):** `MODELS_SETTINGS_PANELS`, `MODELS_SETTINGS_PANEL_LABELS`, `MODELS_SETTINGS_LOCATION`, `modelsSettingsLocation(panel, librarySection?)`, `modelsSettingsPlaceLabel()`, `modelsSettingsSubTab()` / `parseModelsSettingsSubTab()` / `MODELS_SETTINGS_SUB_TAB_PATHS`, `taskAssignmentPanel(taskId)`, `taskAssignmentLocation(taskId)` and `systemSettingsPanel(system)` in `src/shared/local-models/settings-location.ts`. The Models settings tab opens on an Overview (the Models row itself) and has five more panels (`MODELS_SETTINGS_DETAIL_PANELS`, a subtree under Models in the Settings sidebar, moved by `useModelsSettingsNav()` in `src/renderer/components/Settings/tabs/LocalModelsTab/useModelsSettingsNav.ts`), and Library has sections. A place is ONE string, the sub-tab path (`gateway`, `library/speech`): what `maestro-cli open --sub-tab`, a search result's `subTab` and `openSettings('models', undefined, subTab)` all carry. Never split or join that path by hand. The renderer draws the panels from these ids and labels, and every recovery sentence in the main process, the CLI and the renderer ("Assign it a model in Settings > Models > ...") builds its place here. A renamed tab, or a task that moves between System One and System Two, is one edit in this file. Never write the string `Settings > Models` into a message by hand.
- **Cards of the Models settings tab:** `<CardButton theme icon label onClick disabled primary? testId>` in `src/renderer/components/Settings/tabs/LocalModelsTab/CardButton.tsx` (the small bordered action button of the Library's cards; `primary` marks the action that commits the card) and `<CardRowTitle>` / `<CardWarning>` in `CardParts.tsx` beside it (a titled row and a warning line, so a warning reads the same on the Model server card, the System One engine card and the Hugging Face card). A new card in this tab composes these; it does not restyle a bordered button.
- **A model the user adds from Hugging Face:** the store and the one implementation in `src/main/local-models/user-models.ts` (`previewHuggingFaceModel(repo, file)`, `addHuggingFaceModel(repo, file)`, `removeUserLocalModel(id)`, `listUserLocalModels()`, `onUserLocalModelsChanged()`, `hydrateUserLocalModels()`), the pure rules in `src/shared/local-models/user-models.ts` (`parseHuggingFaceRepoId()`, `parseHuggingFaceFileName()`, `userLocalModelId()`, `huggingFaceModelApiUrl()`, `huggingFaceModelRecord()`, `userLocalModelEntry()`, `parseUserLocalModels()`, `USER_MODEL_UNVERIFIED_LABEL`, `USER_LOCAL_MODELS_FILE`), and the per-process registry in `src/shared/local-models/model-catalog.ts` (`setUserLocalModels()`, `userLocalModels()`, `allLocalModels()`, `isCuratedLocalModelId()`). `LOCAL_MODEL_CATALOG` stays the frozen curated table; a user entry is data on this machine (`userData/local-models-user.json`, beside the models root so "remove all" does not delete it). `getLocalModel()`, `isLocalModelId()` and `localModelsForRuntime()` read both, so a user model is installed, verified, served and assigned through the code a curated one is: iterate `allLocalModels()`, never `LOCAL_MODEL_CATALOG`, when a user model should be included. Each process must be TOLD its user entries: main hydrates synchronously at startup (every catalog lookup is synchronous), a window through `startUserLocalModelsSync()` in `src/renderer/services/userLocalModels.ts`, the CLI through `loadUserLocalModels()` in `src/cli/services/storage.ts` (a `preAction` hook on `models`). Adding makes ONE metadata request and downloads nothing; the revision is the repo's commit at that moment and each hash is the LFS SHA-256 Hugging Face reports, which proves the bytes match what Hugging Face described and nothing more, so every surface shows "Not verified by Maestro". Refused by name: a missing, private or gated repo, a file with no reported hash, a projector, a non-GGUF file, an incomplete split set. The entry has no role and an ESTIMATED KV layout (`estimateKvLayout()`, `memoryHint.estimated`). The stored file and the model card are untrusted text: every field is checked, a failing record is dropped, the license link must be https and its name is cut to printable ASCII (it reaches a terminal). A user entry can never take a curated id.
- **Where model files are downloaded from (a Hugging Face mirror):** the `localModelsDownloadBase` setting (default `https://huggingface.co`), spelled by the pure rules in `src/shared/local-models/download-base.ts`: `downloadBaseProblem(raw)` (why a value is unusable, or null; empty and `default` mean the default), `normalizeDownloadBase(raw)`, `resolveDownloadBase(stored)` (a stored value that fails validation reads as the default), `rebaseSourceUrl(sourceUrl, base)`, `isMirrorDownloadBase()`, `downloadSourceName()`. The main process reads it per request through `currentDownloadBase()` in `src/main/local-models/download-base.ts`; `maestro-cli models config set download-base` validates with `downloadBaseProblem()`. The Sources panel no longer exposes a mirror card. The catalog keeps its Hugging Face URLs: the host is swapped at request time by `ModelDownloader.transferFile()` and `fetchRepoInfo()`, never by editing a catalog entry, and the hash check stays mandatory. Do NOT hand-build a `huggingface.co` URL in a new fetch path: take the base from `currentDownloadBase()`.
- **A git query the UI polls in the background:** `execGitReadOnly()`, `isGitTimeout()`, `READ_ONLY_GIT_TIMEOUT_MS` in `src/main/utils/remote-git.ts`. Anything that runs git on a timer (the git indicator's `git:status`, `git:numstat`, `git:branch` and `git:info`) goes through it rather than `execGit()`. A repo in an iCloud Drive or other file-provider folder blocks every `git status` while the OS downloads offloaded files, and a bare `execGit()` has no timeout and no in-flight guard, so each poll tick added one more stuck process - a reporter killed 124 of them (#1739). It does three things: sets `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` so a background read never takes `.git/index.lock` from the agent's own git (the env var, not `--no-optional-locks`, because the flag is a hard error before git 2.15 and an older SSH host would turn it into an empty status that reads as clean); kills the child (local `git` or the `ssh` invocation) after the timeout; and single-flights per SSH remote + folder + argument list, so a caller arriving mid-query joins it. Each caller settles by its own deadline, but the in-flight entry is held until the child actually EXITS, so a process that ignores its kill signal still blocks new spawns: the worst case is one process per folder per query, never one per tick. A timeout comes back as `exitCode: 'ETIMEDOUT'` with empty stdout, which is NOT an answer - the handlers add `timedOut: true` to the reply and the renderer keeps its last good value for that folder rather than showing a clean tree. Killing the local `ssh` does not reach a git already running on the remote host. One-shot reads the user is waiting on (diff, log, show) stay on `execGit()`.
- **SSH remote lookup:** `getSshRemoteById()` in `src/main/stores/getters.ts`
- **Yielding to the event loop during long main-process work:** `yieldToEventLoop()` in `src/main/utils/concurrency.ts`. Synchronous work in the main process (parsing a batch of files, reading SQLite stores one by one, decoding parquet rows) holds the one thread that answers every IPC call, so a long loop yields between batches with `await yieldToEventLoop()` (one `setImmediate` turn). The parquet scan and the Antigravity session listing each carried their own copy of the one-line promise; call this instead. It does not make the work concurrent and it is not a timer: it only lets queued I/O and IPC run before the next batch.
- **Platform detection:** `isWindows()`, `isMacOS()`, `isLinux()` in `src/shared/platformDetection.ts` (works in both processes). In renderer-only code prefer `isWindowsPlatform()`, `isMacOSPlatform()`, `isLinuxPlatform()` in `src/renderer/utils/platformUtils.ts`. Never read `process.platform` in the renderer: the `process` polyfill (`src/renderer/public/process-shim.js`) reports the sentinel `'browser'`, and `navigator.platform` is deprecated and unreliable. Those helpers describe the CLIENT. To ask what the machine Maestro runs on can install or run (a macOS-only feature, for example), use `hostPlatform()` in the same file: in a web-desktop browser the client is some other device, so it answers `''` and a platform-restricted first-party feature is not offered there (`isFirstPartyPluginAvailableOn()` in `src/shared/plugins/first-party.ts`).
- **Encore Feature flags and their defaults:** `ENCORE_FEATURE_DEFAULTS`, `resolveEncoreFeatures(raw)`, `isEncoreFeatureEnabled()`, `requireEncoreFeature()`, `encoreDisabledMessage()` in `src/shared/encoreFeatureDefaults.ts`. Four processes read these flags (the renderer store, `SETTINGS_METADATA`, the main-process Cue/stats gates, and `maestro-cli encore`), and each one used to carry its own copy of the defaults while treating a missing key as `false`. That is not a cosmetic duplicate: a feature that ships ON reads OFF in the main process until something persists the object, so on a fresh install the UI showed Cue running while the stats lineage writer and the telemetry gate for the same run were silently disabled. Resolve every read through `resolveEncoreFeatures()` - it merges onto the defaults and only lets a real boolean override one, so a settings file written before a flag existed keeps that flag's default instead of falling to `undefined` -> false. Its keys are derived from the defaults object, never hand-listed, so a newly graduated feature cannot be missed by the merge. A capability ships off (a plugin) and its entry here flips to `true` when it graduates to an Encore Feature; do NOT re-declare the literal anywhere else. A handler gate reads one flag with `isEncoreFeatureEnabled(settingsStore, flag)` and refuses with `requireEncoreFeature(settingsStore, flag, 'XDisabled')`, never `ef.x === true` or `ef.x !== false` on the raw object. A feature's background services (watchers, timers, sockets, child processes) start and stop through `createEncoreFeatureLifecycle()` in `src/main/encore-feature-lifecycle.ts`, driven by the one `encoreFeatures` store change: `maestro-cli encore enable|disable` is a plain settings write that never reaches the first-party bridge, so a per-feature listener or a bridge-only stop leaves a CLI-disabled feature running until relaunch.
- **Whether a stored agent is live (a switched-off Pianola is dormant):** `filterLiveAgents(agents, encoreFeatures)`, `isAgentDormant(agent, encoreFeatures)`, `isPianolaEnabled()`, `DORMANT_PIANOLA_MESSAGE` in `src/shared/agentVisibility.ts`, bound per process as `getLiveSessions()` (main, `src/main/stores/getters.ts`), `readLiveSessions()` (CLI, `src/cli/services/storage.ts`), and `useLiveSessions()` / `getLiveSessionsSnapshot()` (renderer, `src/renderer/hooks/session/useLiveSessions.ts`). The Pianola agent is a real `isPianola` Session that stays in the store after its Encore flag is switched off, so turning the feature back on restores it. Every surface that read the raw session list kept acting on it while the feature was off: its home-folder `cwd` blocked unrelated folders, name resolution in `send` / `dispatch` / `ask` still found it, Cue watched it, group chat offered it for @mention, Director's Notes summarized it, and git polled its folder. Anything that lists, resolves, watches, polls, or counts agents goes through these helpers (both accept the raw or resolved `encoreFeatures`, and `filterLiveAgents()` returns the same array when nothing is dormant). Persistence, store migrations, and pruning that keys off the full list must keep reading the raw store, or they would delete the dormant agent's data. The renderer's `sessionVisibility.ts` (`filterSessionsVisibleInSidebar()`) delegates to the same rule.
- **A maestro-cli verb that belongs to an Encore Feature:** `encoreCommand(parent, name, flag, flags)`, `requireEncore(flag, json?)`, `requireEncoreForOptions(flag, options, owned)`, `allowWhileEncoreOff(command)` in `src/cli/utils/encore-gate.ts`. A switched-off feature has no footprint in the CLI: `encoreCommand` registers the verb hidden (absent from `--help`, `reference` and `completions`) and adds a preAction hook that refuses every verb beneath it, invoked directly or not, with the one line that turns it back on (`<Name> is turned off. Turn it on in Settings > Plugins, or run: maestro-cli encore enable <flag>`; `--json` gets `<FLAG>_DISABLED`). The hook applies the verb's own `--data-dir` first, so a headless data directory is judged by its own settings. `requireEncoreForOptions` covers a core verb whose options belong to a feature (Groups+ on `create-group --parent`). Before this, each feature carried its own check: Pianola's told the user to run a command that does not exist, Director's Notes read a never-saved flag as off although it ships on, and Computer History reads warned and carried on. Read flags through `readEncoreFlags()` (never `flags?.x === true`), keep `encore` itself ungated (it is how a hidden feature is turned on), and reserve `allowWhileEncoreOff` for a verb that only takes a footprint away, such as `cue engine stop`. `reference --all` (what `npm run gen:cli-reference` runs) still documents every verb. The turned-off text comes from `encoreDisabledMessage(flag)` (with `encoreFeatureName()` and `encoreFeatureCliName()`) in `src/shared/encoreFeatureDefaults.ts`, re-exported here, so a main-process service that refuses with readable text (the Dataview service) says exactly what a gated verb prints.
- **Filing GitHub feedback (check gh, search, +1, submit):** the service functions in `src/main/feedback/index.ts` (`checkFeedbackGhAuth`, `searchFeedbackIssues`, `subscribeFeedbackIssue`, `submitFeedbackConversation`, `buildFeedbackConversationPrompt`). The `feedback:*` IPC handlers and the WS bridge (`maestro-cli feedback`) are both thin wrappers over them, so the CLI files the byte-identical issue the Send Feedback modal does. Wire contract, category aliases and attachment limits are in `src/shared/feedback.ts`. Do NOT put feedback logic back inside `ipcMain.handle` bodies - that is what made it unreachable from the CLI.
- **Writing lifetime counters that must never go down:** `mergeUsagePeaks()` in `src/shared/usagePeaks.ts` (`usageStats`) and `mergeAutoRunStats()` in `src/shared/autoRunStatsMerge.ts` (`autoRunStats`: Conductor time, the Cue share, runs, longest run, badge levels, badge history), both applied by `settings:set` in `src/main/ipc/handlers/persistence.ts` against the value on DISK. Every crediting path read-modify-writes the whole object from a renderer's in-memory copy, and that copy is the zeroed default in a renderer still hydrating, one whose settings load failed, a second window, or a web-desktop client. One Cue credit applied there persisted `{ cumulativeTimeMs: 60000, totalRuns: 0 }` over 1,352 hours and level 6. The merge is the invariant; the renderer-side gates (hold Cue credit until `settingsLoaded`, credit only in `useIsLifecycleOwner()`) only keep the display honest. A new lifetime counter gets the same treatment in the same handler. Do NOT add a renderer-only max: a peer's copy can be staler than yours.

## Spawning and stopping agents (process, SSH, env, prompts)

- **A secret a server supplies by name:** `lookupSecret(name, options?)`, `resolveSecrets(names, options?)`, `isValidSecretName()`, `describeSecretProblem()` in `src/shared/serverSecrets.ts`. ONE lookup for every place a server secret is read: Cue and CLI agent launches (`buildAgentLaunchPlan`'s `requiredSecrets`, scoped to the agent record's `requiredSecrets`), webhook `secret_env`, and the `bundle import` secrets report. Order is `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY/<NAME>` (systemd), then `/run/secrets/<NAME>` (Docker/Kubernetes), then the environment: a file is the deliberate channel and must not be shadowed by a stale env value, and a file that EXISTS but is unusable (unreadable, a directory, empty, over 64 KiB) is reported rather than falling through. Names must be env var names, which is also the traversal guard; one trailing line ending is dropped. Values go into one child's environment and nowhere else - never `process.env` (that hands every secret to every agent), never the plan's `envVars` (shown in Process Details, crosses to SSH), never a log, error message, database row or status payload; every report it returns names and paths only. Do NOT read `process.env[secretName]` directly for a server secret, and do NOT add a second file lookup: the readiness gate and anything else that asks "is this secret available?" must answer it the way the launch will. The desktop surface never reads secret files.
- **maestro-lib as a library for a program outside this repository:** `src/shared/maestro-lib/index.ts`, with `MAESTRO_LIB_VERSION` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/version.ts`. The entry re-exports what an outside program needs to plan, run, stream, resume and stop a turn and read its outcome, plus the parsers and the host hooks; everything else under `src/shared/maestro-lib/` is internal. `npm run build:maestro-lib` bundles it into `dist/maestro-lib/` (CommonJS, declarations, a private `package.json` carrying the version, and `docs/maestro-lib.md` as its README). The user-facing documentation is `docs/maestro-lib.md`; do not restate it here or in a README beside the code. **Who imports through it:** code outside this repository imports only the entry. Code inside the repository still reaches into deep paths and is being moved onto the entry one caller at a time, so before moving a caller onto the entry, check that every symbol it imports from a deep path is already exported here. No test enforces this; the source-scanning drift guard that used to was removed on `rc`. The entry carries the turn API this entry was built for and, since 0.3.0, the headless runtime's surface (paths and doctor, the lock primitive and data-dir lock, the in-process runtime and `MaestroClient`, store readers, Auto Run, group chat and consults). **Changing the surface:** add or remove the export in `index.ts`, bump `MAESTRO_LIB_VERSION` (patch for a fix, minor for an addition, major for a removal; below 1.0 a minor may break), and update `docs/maestro-lib.md`. `src/shared/maestro-lib/__tests__/docs-examples.test.ts` type-checks every `ts` block on that page against the entry and compares its provider table, refusal reasons and version with the library, `src/shared/maestro-lib/__tests__/built-entry.test.ts` loads the built bundle in plain Node, and `src/shared/maestro-lib/__tests__/no-desktop-framework.smoke.test.ts` walks the entry's import graph for Electron and `src/main`. A gap the terminal UI finds is fixed in the library and exported here, never worked around in the UI by reaching past the entry.
- **Escaping for a Windows cmd.exe launch:** `escapeCmdArgs()`, `windowsShellReason()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/windows-shell-escape.ts` (the escapers) and `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/windows-command.ts` (when a shell is needed). Not the POSIX `shellEscape()`. Node refuses to start an npm `.cmd` shim without a shell (`spawn EINVAL`), so `windowsShellReason()` decides when the shell is needed, `quoteCommandForCmdShell()` quotes a command path with spaces, and `escapeCmdArgs()` / `escapeArgsForShell()` escape the arguments. The desktop spawners import them through `src/main/process-manager/utils/shellEscape.ts`, which re-exports them, so their tests mock that path unchanged. For a turn spec, `applyWindowsShellRules(spec)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/start-turn.ts` applies all three and is a no-op for a spec that already names its shell. The escaper doubles `^` inside quotes and cannot stop `%NAME%` expansion or a line break, so a prompt bound for a shell launch goes over stdin; `cmdShellArgProblem()` names an argument cmd.exe cannot carry.
- **Launching an agent (env layers, prompt delivery, SSH target):** `buildAgentLaunchPlan()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/launch-plan.ts`, over `buildAgentEnvironment()` / `resolveAgentEnvVars()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/env.ts`, `resolvePromptDelivery()` / `resolveSystemPromptDelivery()` / `buildPromptArgv()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/prompt-delivery.ts`, and `resolveSshLaunchTarget()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/ssh-remote-resolver.ts`. Desktop chat (`handle-spawn.ts`), Cue (`cue-spawn-builder.ts`) and the CLI (`agent-spawner.ts`) each build their own ARGUMENTS, and everything after that is decided here. **One place is not one behavior.** The three surfaces layer an agent's environment differently and deliver its prompt differently, and did so before this module existed; the caller names its `surface` (`AgentEnvSurface`: `'desktop' | 'cli' | 'cue'`) and the plan reproduces that surface exactly. Desktop: `process.env` with the Electron/IDE vars stripped `< globalShellEnvVars < defaultEnvVars < (sessionCustomEnvVars ?? agentCustomEnvVars) < readOnlyEnvOverrides < Maestro-stated vars < MAESTRO_QUERY_SOURCE`, `~/` expanded, a blank value unsets the variable, `MAESTRO_SESSION_RESUMED` stamped on a resume. CLI: `process.env` as inherited with PATH expanded; `defaultEnvVars` and `batchModeEnvVars` fill ONLY what the shell left unset (a user who exported the variable in the shell that runs the command keeps it), then the user's vars, the read-only overrides and the query source override; values are written as given; the global Settings vars are not applied. Cue: `process.env` as inherited with the desktop spawn PATH `< defaultEnvVars < user vars < read-only < MAESTRO_QUERY_SOURCE`; no global Settings vars. Making the three agree (global above the defaults, stripping Electron's vars everywhere) is a PRODUCT change with its own regressions, for example a CLI user's exported `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` silently losing to the default, so it is proposed on its own and never folded into a refactor of this module. The session record REPLACES the provider-level one rather than layering over it on every surface, the rule `effectiveAgentCustomEnvVars()` and `resolveAgentEnvironment()` share, so usage attribution keeps describing the process that actually runs. `resolveAgentEnvVars()` is the record Maestro sets, WITHOUT `process.env` and WITHOUT the global Settings vars: what crosses to an SSH remote and what Process Details lists. Desktop's SSH wrapper (`wrap-spawn-for-ssh.ts`) still merges the global Settings vars beneath that record into the remote environment, as `rc` does; keeping them off the remote (a global path names a directory on this machine, not on the remote) would be a behavior change of its own. **An unresolvable SSH remote is an error, never a local run:** `resolveSshLaunchTarget()` names the reason (no remote selected, deleted, disabled, no remote list) in a message fit to show as-is, and the plan fails before any side effect. This is the one deliberate behavior change in the plan: desktop and Cue used to run the agent LOCALLY against the remote's path, because `getSshRemoteConfig()` folds all of those cases into "local". **Prompt delivery follows the surface too**: desktop moves the prompt to stdin on a Windows host for an agent that declared `supportsPromptViaStdin` (omp exits 0 with no output when handed stdin); the CLI and Cue keep it on the command line on every host; stream-json when images ride along; the SSH script on a remote. Desktop re-runs `resolvePromptDelivery()` on its FINAL prompt, after the system prompt and preambles are folded in. Do NOT hand-roll another `promptArgs ? ... : noPromptSeparator ? ... : ['--', prompt]` chain (there were five) or another env merge at a spawn site.
- **Whether a prompt fits on the command line:** `promptExceedsArgvLimit(text, windowsHost)`, `windowsCommandLineLength(command, args)`, `windowsQuotedArgLength(arg)`, `WINDOWS_PROMPT_ARGV_MAX_CHARS`, `POSIX_PROMPT_ARGV_MAX_BYTES` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/prompt-delivery.ts`. A prompt too long for argv cannot start at all: Windows CreateProcess caps the WHOLE command line near 32K characters (`spawn ENAMETOOLONG`), Linux caps a SINGLE argument at 128 KiB and macOS caps argv plus env at 1 MiB (`spawn E2BIG`). The two constants leave headroom under those caps and are measured in DIFFERENT units on purpose: the Windows cap counts UTF-16 code units AFTER Node quotes the argument, the POSIX cap counts UTF-8 bytes, so a prompt of multi-byte text crosses the POSIX line at a third of its character count. **Windows is measured as libuv builds the line, never as written:** `windowsQuotedArgLength` mirrors libuv's `quote_cmd_arg` exactly (an argument with a space, tab or `"` is wrapped in quotes, every `"` gains a backslash, and backslashes before a `"` or the closing quote are doubled), so a prompt of JSON well under 30,000 characters can still overrun CreateProcess. Both the prompt check and the SSH line check go through it, so they cannot disagree; do NOT compare a raw `.length` against the Windows cap. `windowsHost` is the OS of the machine that SPAWNS the process, never a client's: a web-desktop browser can sit on another OS than the host. Every place that decides argv versus stdin asks this one function: `resolvePromptDelivery()` (desktop, Cue, CLI; its `hostIsWindows` input is what lets Cue and the CLI keep a short Windows prompt in argv yet move an over-long one, MAESTRO-16E), `prepareGroupChatSpawn()` (moderator, participant, synthesis, consult), and `wrapSpawnWithSsh()` for the inline ssh command line. Only an agent that declares `supportsPromptViaStdin` is moved to stdin; any other keeps its prompt in argv, because an over-long command line fails loudly while stdin to a CLI that ignores it fails silently (omp exits 0 with no output). **An SSH command has two ends.** The inline `ssh host '<cmd>'` form puts the whole remote command in ONE local argument, and sshd hands it to the remote login shell as one `-c` argument, so off Windows the check is per argument in bytes (which covers both ends) and on Windows it is `windowsCommandLineLength` of the whole line (far tighter than the remote cap). Do NOT apply the Windows number on every host: that is what sent every Linux and macOS SSH consult carrying the ~35 KB system prompt through the stdin script, losing the inline path's forced `-tt`, for a command that would have started fine. `cue-cli-executor.ts` reads the same two constants for the message it passes to `maestro-cli send` as argv; it truncates by characters, so there the POSIX number is applied as a character count, as it always was. Do NOT declare another 30,000 / 100,000 pair. Known gap: `groomContext()` in `src/main/utils/context-groomer.ts` still moves a prompt to stdin only on Windows.
- **Starting, streaming, stopping and resuming an agent turn:** `startTurn(spec, handlers, options)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/start-turn.ts`, `runTurn()` / `runToCompletion()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/run-to-completion.ts` over `TurnCapture` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/turn-capture.ts`, and `planSessionTurn()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/session.ts`, with the headless program in `src/shared/maestro-lib/bin/run-turn.ts`. Four pieces of code used to own a process each: desktop's `ChildProcessSpawner`, the CLI's `spawnClaudeAgent` and `spawnJsonLineAgent`, and Cue's `runProcess`, and no two shared an implementation of "start it, deliver the prompt, decode stdout on the stream, split it into lines, parse each one, stop it, report how it ended". `startTurn` is that implementation, and all four now start through it. Desktop passes the stream on unparsed (`onStdout`) to its own `StdoutHandler`, which knows four framings the run layer does not (newline-delimited JSON, Copilot's concatenated objects, batch JSON, raw text), and emits every renderer event from those callbacks in the order it always did: `raw-stdout`, then the handler; `stderr`; then `exit`. It keeps stdin OPEN for an interactive process (`keepStdinOpen`), because `ProcessManager.write()` talks to it later, and it runs the command through a shell on Windows when `windowsShellReason()` says so (`spec.shell`). One consequence of settling through `done`: a provider that fails to start is reported ONCE. Node follows ENOENT's `error` with a `close`, and the old spawner ran both handlers, so the renderer heard two `exit` events for one failed spawn. It takes a `TurnProcessSpec` (command, args, cwd, env, and text for stdin), which is the last word after planning and any SSH or wrapper step; a local `AgentLaunchPlan` becomes one through `turnProcessSpecFromPlan()`, a remote plan through the SSH wrapper first. What it guarantees: every handler for a chunk has returned before the next chunk is read, and every handler has returned before `done` resolves, so a caller that settles on `done` has seen it all; the last line is flushed even without a trailing newline; stdout is decoded on the stream, so a multibyte character split across two reads survives; `interrupt()` / `terminate()` / `terminateNow()` run the shared stop ladder and mark the turn `interrupted` BEFORE the first signal; an `AbortSignal` does the same from `terminate`; a command that does not exist is reported through `done` as `spawnError` rather than thrown, because the OS only says so after `spawn` returned. It decides nothing about SUCCESS: the caller resolves `TurnExit` with `resolveTurnOutcome` and its own options, and keeps its own timeouts. Three things are opt-in because callers disagreed: `maxLineLength` (the CLI caps a stuck line at 1 MB, Cue never did), `emptyStdin: 'ignore'` (Cue gives a child the null device, because Codex `exec` announces it is waiting on stdin before it notices a closed pipe), and how much of each stream `TurnExit` keeps (`stdoutTailLimit`, `stderrTailLimit`): the CLI keeps all of stderr because it prints it as the error, `runTurn` bounds both, and desktop and Cue pass `0` because they hold their own copy and a second one would only grow for as long as the process lives. An error on the child's stdin (EPIPE, from a prompt written to a process that already went) is handled inside `startTurn` and handed to `onStdinError`; with no listener it is an uncaught exception that takes the host down. When it is the prompt's own write that failed, `TurnExit.stdinError` keeps it and `resolveTurnOutcome` reports the turn `crashed` (rule 3b), because a clean exit after an undelivered prompt is not a finished turn; a later write by a caller that keeps stdin open is not charged to the turn. `TurnCapture` is what a turn's parsed events add up to (first session id announced, result text else streamed deltas, usage combined by `usageReportingFor(agentId)`: Codex's running total becomes deltas, Claude's terminal result wins outright, everyone else sums), and it classifies in-band failures unless told not to; the CLI's generic path passes `classifyInBandErrors: false` so its outcomes stay exactly what they were, while its Claude path keeps its own raw-message reader because Claude's `modelUsage` is richer than the parser's usage event. `runTurn` is the buffered form for a caller that only wants the finished turn. `planSessionTurn` looks the provider up, finds its binary, builds batch arguments with resume through `buildAgentArgs`, and plans the launch as the CLI does, local only. It refuses, before anything starts, what the runner could not deliver: a provider with no output parser (the turn would run and its answer could not be read) and a read-only turn for a provider whose CLI cannot enforce one (`readOnlyCliEnforced`), because a program with nobody watching must get read-only or a refusal. `maestro-lib-run` is that as a program: `npm run build` builds it, `bin` registers it, the packaged app carries it in its resources beside `maestro-p.js`, its test bundles it with esbuild and runs it under plain `node`, and it pauses the agent's stdout while its own is full, so a slow reader cannot grow it without bound. Do NOT add another `spawn()` + `stdout.on('data')` loop at an agent call site, and do NOT put a completion rule inside `startTurn`.
- **Stopping a process (interrupt, terminate, kill the tree):** `stopProcess(target, options)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/control/termination.ts`, over `snapshotProcessTree()` / `refreshProcessTree()` / `killSurvivors()` / `killProcessTreeNow()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/control/process-tree.ts` and `killPty()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/control/pty-kill.ts`. One ladder for every caller: SIGINT (Ctrl+C on stdin or the PTY on Windows), then SIGTERM or `taskkill /t /f`, then SIGKILL for the whole tree. Desktop (`ProcessManager.interrupt()` / `kill()`), Cue (`killCueProcess()`), the CLI (`linkAbortSignal()`) and the pianola supervisor each carried a copy of the ladder, and the copies had drifted on every part of it. Desktop's interrupt decided whether to escalate by reading `child.killed`, which Node sets as soon as a signal is SENT, so on POSIX an agent that ignored SIGINT was never escalated and the tab stayed busy. Desktop's kill never followed SIGTERM with SIGKILL for a pipe child, the CLI had no Windows tree kill, and `taskkill /t /f` was spelled out in six files. None of them stopped what the agent had started: an OpenCode turn stopped mid-tool exits on the signal and leaves the tool running as an orphan, still doing its work with nothing left to stop it (measured on a real OpenCode: the turn itself closes, the `sleep` its shell tool started keeps running). The rules the module holds so no caller restates them: stop is recorded (`onStopRequested`) BEFORE the first signal, so the exit resolves as `interrupted` and not as a crash caused by our own SIGTERM; a process is running while `exitCode` and `signalCode` are both null; every stage schedules the next, for pipes and PTYs alike; descendants are recorded BEFORE the first signal (a child is re-parented the moment its parent dies and no longer leads back to it), re-read every `TREE_REFRESH_MS` while the stop is pending (an agent that is winding down can still start a tool after the first record was taken) and swept once the process exits; and a pid is only signalled when its start time still matches the recording, because seconds pass between the two and a freed pid can be handed to an unrelated process. A tree is only killed when the pid is provably a running child of this process (`ProcessTreeSnapshot.owned`); anything else gets the signal through its own handle and nothing more, which is also what keeps a test's made-up pid from ever reaching a real process. Where the record comes from depends on the platform: macOS asks `ps -o lstart`, Linux reads `/proc/<pid>/stat` directly (BusyBox `ps`, on Alpine and in many containers, cannot report `lstart`, and the tick count in `/proc` is an exact identity), and Windows records nothing because `taskkill /t` walks the tree itself. Agents are found by PARENT LINK, never by process group: real OpenCode and real Claude Code both run a tool in its OWN group, so a group kill of the agent's group returns ESRCH and reaches nothing. One ladder runs per process: a second `stopProcess` on the same target (Stop pressed twice, a tab closed while a Stop is pending) advances the ladder already running instead of stacking a second set of timers. `from` and `graceMs` are required so each caller STATES its choice: a user Stop starts at `interrupt`, a closed tab, a timeout or an aborted run at `terminate`, and the grace is `INTERACTIVE_STOP_GRACE_MS` (2 s) for a turn someone is watching or `BACKGROUND_STOP_GRACE_MS` (5 s) for an unattended run. `immediate` is the shutdown path (every remaining stage at once, no timer, no exit listener, per MAESTRO-3B), `blocking` waits for `taskkill` so the tree is gone before the host exits, and `upTo` caps the ladder at a stage. **Quitting the desktop app sends what it always has**, through `upTo`: a pipe-backed agent gets SIGTERM and nothing behind it, so it can finish writing its transcript or session file (a SIGKILL sent right after would take that away, and at quit there is no event loop left to wait out a grace period), a PTY gets SIGKILL, Windows gets a blocking `taskkill`, and descendants are left alone. When `taskkill` fails for a child that is still running, the child is ended through its own handle, so a stop never leaves it running. Two things are deliberately NOT routed through the ladder. A PTY interrupt stays a bare Ctrl+C with no escalation, because a PTY process that survives Ctrl+C is the normal case (a shell that cancelled a command, a TUI that cancelled its turn and is waiting for input). And a terminal tab passes `includeDescendants: false`, because a job the user left running in their own shell is theirs to keep. `taskkill` is called directly, never through a shell. Do NOT hand-roll another `kill('SIGTERM')` + `setTimeout` pair at a spawn site, and do NOT read `child.killed` to decide anything.
- **Whether a Claude turn runs the maestro-p TUI or `claude --print`:** `resolveClaudeSpawnModeCore()` / `applyClaudeSpawnDecision()` / `buildRemoteInteractiveSpawn()` / `isMaestroPBinaryPath()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/interactive-mode.ts`, over `selectMode()` in `launch/claude-mode-selector.ts`. One decision function for chat, Auto Run, tab naming, synopsis, group chat and Cue, with the native-backed deps supplied by `src/main/agents/resolveClaudeSpawnMode.ts` and lightweight ones by the CLI. `src/main/agents/claudeSpawnCore.ts` and `claude-mode-selector.ts` are re-exports of it.
- **Timing out a supervised agent process:** `createIdleWatchdog({ idleMs, maxMs?, onIdle, onMax? })` in `src/main/utils/idle-watchdog.ts`, fed by the event list in `src/main/utils/agent-liveness.ts`. **The budget is SILENCE, not wall clock.** A plain `setTimeout` armed at spawn measures TOTAL duration, so an agent emitting output every second and one that died a minute in look identical to it - which is how a Group Chat participant was declared dead at ten minutes while its transcript showed 19-41 events per minute straight through the cutoff. The caller `touch()`es on every proof of life and the clock restarts; the optional `maxMs` ceiling is a SECOND, non-resettable timer, because an agent stuck in a tool loop chatters forever and can never satisfy the idle budget. What counts as proof of life is `AGENT_LIVENESS_EVENTS` (`data`, `thinking-chunk`, `tool-execution`, `usage`), and getting that list wrong silently reintroduces the wall-clock bug: for a `--print` stream-json run `StdoutHandler` emits `data` ONLY on the terminal result, so a budget armed on `data` alone is a hard deadline that never re-arms. Both the cross-agent consult router and the Group Chat router shipped exactly that. `raw-stdout` is deliberately excluded from the shared list (it fires per chunk for every process in the app) and opted into by name where wanted. **A timeout must kill before it reports**: changing only Maestro's state tells the room the turn failed while the process it gave up on goes on editing files, committing, and pushing. Kill with the FULL spawned session id - `getModeratorSessionId()` returns the per-chat PREFIX that every turn appends a timestamp to, and `ProcessManager.kill` matches exact keys, so the prefix kills nothing. Group Chat's budgets are re-armed by `setupGroupChatLivenessListener` (`src/main/process-listeners/`), which hands session ids to `noteGroupChatActivity()`; the router itself never grows an EventEmitter. Do NOT hand-roll another `setTimeout` supervisor for a spawned agent.
- **A one-shot answer from a cheap model (no tab, no tools):** `window.maestro.context.groomContext(projectRoot, agentType, prompt, { cheapTurn, readOnly, disableTools, timeoutMs })` (preload `src/main/preload/context.ts`, handler `src/main/ipc/handlers/context.ts`), over `groomContext()` in `src/main/utils/context-groomer.ts`. This is the app's one generic "prompt in, text out" call: it spawns an ephemeral `groomer-*` session that touches no tab and no conversation, honors the utility agent, SSH remotes, and the agent's own env. For a turn whose output CODE reads (a classification, a one-word decision, as in Quick Chat's tab routing) set all four: `cheapTurn` pins the bottom of the model and effort ladders, `readOnly` and `disableTools` mean nothing can run or be written, and `timeoutMs` replaces the five-minute grooming default with a deadline a waiting user can live with. A timeout with no output KILLS the child (it used to be left running until it finished on its own, which a short deadline would stack up) and rejects, so wrap the call and have a fallback. It resolves with whatever text was buffered on any exit, so validate the answer against the labels you asked for. Do NOT reach for `tabNaming.generateTabName` for this: it wraps the input in the naming template and squeezes the output into a 40-character title. Do NOT set `cheapTurn` for grooming or transfer, whose output becomes the context every later turn reads.
- **Whether a working directory can be spawned into:** `unusableCwdReason(cwd)` in `src/main/process-manager/utils/spawnCwd.ts`, called once by `ProcessManager.spawn()` for both the PTY and child-process paths. A missing `cwd` is an ordinary user state (a deleted worktree, an unmounted volume, a renamed project folder) and it MUST be caught before the spawn, because node-pty's Windows path does not fail anywhere catchable: `pty.spawn()` returns a live-looking object and defers the real work to `WindowsPtyAgent._completePtyConnection()`, which runs later from the conout worker's `onReady` callback (or a 5 s timeout) and calls the native `conptyNative.connect(pty, commandLine, cwd, ...)` there. A `cwd` that is not a directory throws win32 `error 267` (ERROR_DIRECTORY) out of THAT callback - past the `try`/`catch` in `PtySpawner.spawn`, past the IPC handler, into the uncaught-exception handler - while the spawn is reported as successful and the pty is left a zombie with no inner pid, so the caller asks again. Sentry grouped 998 of these crashes under one issue (MAESTRO-S0), 995 of them from a single installation relaunching into the same missing directory. The spawn is REFUSED rather than redirected to the home directory: an agent silently pointed at the wrong tree would read and edit files nobody asked it to. The check is deliberately conservative and only reports a directory it can PROVE is unusable (`ENOENT`/`ENOTDIR`, or a path that stats as a non-directory); any other stat failure returns `null` and lets the spawn proceed, so a locked folder or a flaky network mount can never lock a user out of starting their agent. The refusal emits an `agent-error` naming the directory, so it reaches the user instead of only the log.
- **Whether an agent is working right now (main process):** `isAgentBusy(session, processManager)` and `isAiTabProcessActive(...)` in `src/main/utils/agent-busy.ts`. **Do NOT read `session.state` or `tab.state` to answer this** - `useDebouncedPersistence` forces every session and tab back to `'idle'` before writing, so a stuck busy state cannot survive a restart and the stored record ALWAYS reads idle. Liveness has to come from what is actually running: a managed process keyed by the compound AI id (`{agentId}-ai-{tabId}`, plus the legacy bare `{agentId}-ai` for the ACTIVE tab only), OR `isSessionBusyWithCli()` for a playbook the CLI drives on that agent, OR the host-owned headless process registry in `src/main/utils/headless-agent-activity.ts`. That registry begins only at the spawner's `onProcessStarted` callback and releases each run independently in `finally`; it never assigns a desktop tab. `isAgentBusy` also accepts an optional precomputed CLI-busy set for list projections to avoid rereading the activity file for every agent. Two levels because two questions get asked - `isAiTabProcessActive` is per-tab (what the web server renders as a tab's state), `isAgentBusy` is per-agent ("may I hand this agent more work?", which Group Chat delegation asks). Distinct from `checkAgentBusy()` in `src/cli/services/agent-busy.ts` despite the matching filename: the CLI is a separate process with no `ProcessManager`, so it reads the sessions JSON off disk. Pick by process, and do not import across the two.
- **Shell escape:** `shellEscape()`, `shellEscapeArgs()`, `shellEscapeForDoubleQuotes()` in `src/main/utils/shell-escape.ts`
- **Resuming a provider session a background synopsis may still hold:** `awaitSynopsisWriters()` in `src/main/process-manager/utils/synopsisWriterGate.ts`, with `SYNOPSIS_WRITER_WAIT_MS` / `SYNOPSIS_WRITER_KILL_GRACE_MS`. The exit listener resumes the tab's provider session for a synopsis right after each turn, under a `{id}-synopsis-{ts}` process id that never marks the tab busy, so the tab looks free while a second writer holds its session. A turn sent in that window (queue drain, `maestro-cli dispatch`, a fast follow-up) used to resume the same session concurrently: Codex refuses a second writer outright ("thread already has an active writer") and the user's turn failed; Claude accepts both and interleaves two writers into one JSONL. `process:spawn` (`handle-spawn.ts`) calls the gate for every spawn that carries an `agentSessionId`, waits up to 60s, then kills the synopsis, because the user's turn outranks the History entry. It subscribes to `exit` BEFORE checking the registry, so an exit between scan and subscription cannot cost the full wait. Serializing at the one spawn choke point is the point: do NOT add a busy check on an individual send path, and do NOT mark the tab busy for a synopsis to get the same effect (that lights the Thinking pill for background work).
- **A path handed to a REMOTE shell (`cd`, `ls`, `stat` over SSH):** `shellEscapeRemotePath()` in `src/main/utils/shell-escape.ts`. `shellEscape()` single-quotes, and a single-quoted `'~/proj'` never expands: the remote looks for a directory literally named `~`, and nothing local can substitute the remote user's home (`expandTilde()` produced `/Users/<local>/git-projects`, which validated green and then did not exist on the host). A leading `~` or `$HOME` is rendered as `"$HOME/rest"`; every other path falls through to `shellEscape()` byte for byte. Every remote `cd` rides it - both SSH command builders, the terminal tab handler, `SshCommandRunner`, and all of `remote-fs.ts` - because four private copies of the rule had already drifted, and one of them defaulted to a quoted `'~'` that could not work. Do NOT hand-roll another `startsWith('~/')` branch at a new call site.
- **SSH connection options (`ssh -o`):** `resolveSshOptions()` / `buildSshOptionArgs()` / `validateSshOption()` in `src/shared/sshOptions.ts`. FOUR sites used to build this list by hand - the agent spawn builder, the Test Connection probe, `SshCommandRunner`, and the interactive terminal tab in `ipc/handlers/process.ts` - and they had already drifted: only one set `LogLevel`, and the terminal path omitted `BatchMode` entirely, so a remote could test green and then connect with a different option set when an agent actually ran on it. The merge is CASE-INSENSITIVE, which is the whole reason it is one function: OpenSSH treats keywords case-insensitively but applies `-o` FIRST-WINS, so a naive spread emitting both `-o ConnectTimeout=10` and `-o connecttimeout=45` silently keeps the 10 and the user's override does nothing. A command-line `-o` also outranks `~/.ssh/config`, so `SshRemoteConfig.sshOptions` is the ONLY way a user can change one of these defaults - that is what makes an exotic transport (a tailcat / cloudflared / Teleport `ProxyCommand`, a `ProxyJump` bastion) expressible without a field per transport. `RequestTTY` is RESERVED: it is derived per command from whether the remote agent speaks stream-json, and a forced TTY corrupts that stream, so it is a property of the command and pinning it per host silently breaks agents. The `'interactive'` context drops `BatchMode`, `RequestTTY` and `LogLevel` (the user is at the keyboard and can answer a passphrase prompt, the caller passes `-t`, and SSH's own warnings are the diagnostics someone watching a terminal needs) and an override may NOT reinstate one - adding it back re-breaks the terminal it was dropped for. Do NOT hand-roll another `-o` list; the module is import-free so the CLI bundle can use it too.
- **Building or delivering the Maestro system prompt:** `assembleMaestroSystemPrompt()`, `systemPromptSectionsFor()` in `src/shared/maestroSystemPrompt.ts`; `applySystemPromptDelivery()` in `src/main/utils/system-prompt-delivery.ts`; `buildMaestroSystemPromptForSession()`, `buildPluginSystemPromptSections()` in `src/main/utils/maestro-system-prompt.ts`. Three builders resolve inputs in their own process (renderer `prepareMaestroSystemPrompt`, CLI `prepareMaestroSystemPromptCli`, main `buildMaestroSystemPromptForSession` for Cue, Group Chat, and cross-agent consults) and all hand them to the one assembler, which substitutes the base template and every section with the SAME `TemplateContext` and joins them with `\n\n---\n\n` (role sections such as Pianola first, then plugin sections). Plugin sections come only from `FirstPartyPluginDefinition.systemPromptSection` (community plugins cannot inject; every agent would carry it), gated by the Encore flag through `resolveEncoreFeatures` semantics, and `localOnly` sections are dropped for SSH agents.
