A plain-text paste loses its surrounding whitespace: when trimming changes the text, the native paste is cancelled, the trimmed text replaces the selection in `currentValue`, `setValue` gets the result, and the caret lands after the pasted text on the next frame; otherwise it returns false and the native paste runs untouched. The value is a PARAMETER rather than read off the element so each surface splices into its own source of truth (AI: the textarea's value through `setInputValue`; Group Chat: its `message` state, with `setValue` also persisting the draft). Call it only for a paste without an image. `useInputHandlers.handlePaste` still skips text pastes while a room is active, because the room's own wrapper owns them and only forwards images.
- **Shell tab completion:** `useTabCompletion()` in `src/renderer/hooks/input/useTabCompletion.ts` serves BOTH the terminal composer and command mode, told apart by its third `commandMode` argument (the text can't say which - a command line looks the same either way). Terminal mode completes against `shellCwd` + `shellCommandHistory`; command mode completes against the project root + the bang-prefixed entries in `aiCommandHistory`, because a command-mode command runs at the agent's `cwd` regardless of where a terminal tab has `cd`-ed. Suggestion values are plain command lines in both cases. Add completion sources here, not in a parallel hook.
- **Revealing output the user asked for, past a paused auto-scroll:** `requestTranscriptScrollToBottom(sessionId, tabId)` and `TRANSCRIPT_SCROLL_TO_BOTTOM_EVENT` in `src/renderer/services/transcriptScroll.ts`. The transcript follows new output only while auto-scroll is live; scrolling up to read history pauses it, and from then on new entries land offscreen behind the unread badge. That is correct for output the AGENT produced on its own schedule, and wrong for output the user just asked for by pressing Enter - a bang command's card starts streaming immediately, and a command whose output you cannot see has not really run. `useTerminalOutputScroll` owns the pause in LOCAL state and is mounted several levels below the composer, so the request rides one app-level `CustomEvent`, the same shape as `requestHeadingPalette` and `requestFileTreeRefresh`, rather than a callback drilled up through `MainPanel` and `App`. Four rules make it safe. **Fire it AFTER the content exists** - `runShellCommand` appends the card first, or the transcript scrolls to a bottom that does not include it yet. **Fire it ONCE per run, at the top**, never per output chunk: from there the transcript's own follow-the-tail handles the stream, and a scroll up mid-run pauses it as usual - a per-chunk request would pin the user to the bottom and make the transcript unreadable while a chatty command runs. **The receiver reuses the hook's own `scrollToBottomAndResume()`**, the same path the scroll-to-bottom button takes, which flips `userScrolledAwayRef` / `isAtBottomRef` BEFORE the `setState` calls - the MutationObserver reads the live refs in the same frame and would otherwise stall on the first mutation after the jump. **The detail names its session AND tab**, and a mismatch is ignored, so a command dispatched into a background tab cannot yank the view off what the user is reading. Do NOT reach for this to force ordinary agent output into view - the pause exists precisely to stop that.
- **Appending to a transcript entry:** `canAppendToLogEntry(entry, source)` / `isSelfContainedCard(entry)` in `src/renderer/utils/logEntries.ts`. A `LogEntry` is either an open stream (coalesce consecutive chunks into it) or a self-contained card that owns its own text (`shellCommand`, `retryOutageId`, `recoveryAction`, `aiCommand`, `metadata.hiddenProgress`, `metadata.toolState`). Cards keep a NATURAL source - a `!` command's card is `source: 'stdout'` because its body really is terminal output - so **never gate coalescing on `source` alone**; that's how agent replies ended up rendered inside a command's output box. One card has no marker at all: Claude's plan-limit banner arrives as ordinary `stdout` text on the `result` envelope, after the failure that started the retry was already raised, so `isSelfContainedCard` recognizes it by text via `isClaudeLimitNotice()` (`src/shared/agentErrorPatterns.ts`) - which is anchored and length-capped, so an agent merely _discussing_ limits still coalesces normally. Without that, the answer the retry produces half an hour later renders with "You've hit your session limit - resets ..." glued to its front, and transcripts already on disk are fixed too. Adding a new card kind means adding its marker to `isSelfContainedCard`, which fixes every coalescing site at once.
- **`{{template}}` variable autocomplete:** the shared state machine `useTemplateAutocompleteEngine()` in `src/renderer/hooks/input/useTemplateAutocompleteEngine.ts`, plus ONE binding per text surface - `useTemplateAutocomplete()` for a plain `<textarea>` (the command panels, the prompt composers) and `useEditorTemplateAutocomplete()` for the CodeMirror `MarkdownEditor` (Maestro Prompts, Auto Run). The two surfaces share nothing at the DOM level (a textarea has `selectionStart` and no per-character boxes, so its caret is located with a mirror div; CM6 has neither and reports coordinates from the view), but everything ABOVE the DOM is identical - when to open, what the query is, which key does what, and what text replaces what - so that half lives in the engine and each surface supplies a three-method `TemplateAutocompleteTarget` (`getCaret`, `getCaretPosition`, `replaceRange`), all of them about caret positions. Do NOT hand-roll a second `{{`-detector against a new editor: the copies drift on the parts that are subtle, namely that backing out past the trigger or typing `}}` by hand must CLOSE the popup, and that `selectedIndex` has to be re-clamped as the filter shrinks the list. The target is read through a ref rather than listed as a dependency, because its host rebuilds it every render and naming it would churn every callback's identity on each keystroke. On CM6 the binding must also return `true` from `onKeyDown` to claim Up/Down/Enter/Escape, or the editor's own keymap moves the caret first.
- **Sending a prompt to an agent from outside the composer:** `enqueuePromptForTab()` / `buildQueuedMessageItem()` in `src/renderer/services/queuedPrompt.ts`. Anything that asks an agent a question without a user typing it (the CLI's `dispatch --queue`, a snooze's wake prompt) has the same two problems: the item must be byte-identical to one the composer would have built, and it must not race whatever the agent is already doing. **Queue it, do not spawn it.** The queue answers both - `useQueueProcessing` drains an idle agent on its next render and a busy one when its turn ends, so no caller re-implements "is the agent free?", and the target tab is re-resolved at DRAIN time (`resolveQueuedItemTarget`), which is what makes this safe to call in the same tick as the store write that created the tab. Firing `maestro:remoteCommand` there instead reads a render-time `sessionsRef` and silently drops the prompt. Three fields are easy to omit and each has a visible cost: `tabName` (what a closed tab's item falls back to), `readOnlyMode` (what lets it bypass the parallel-execution guard), and `turnSettings` via `captureQueuedTurnSettings` (what keeps a queued turn on the model it was queued with). The `@mention` flags are STAMPED here and fired by `processQueuedItem` - see the entry above. Module functions, not a hook, because the callers are a sweep timer and an IPC listener. See [RENDERER-SERVICES.md](RENDERER-SERVICES.md#queuedpromptts---send-a-prompt-without-a-composer).
- **Running or withdrawing one queued item now:** `sendQueuedItemNow(sessionId, itemId, dispatch?)` and `removeQueuedItem(sessionId, itemId)` in `src/renderer/services/queuedPrompt.ts`. `sendQueuedItemNow` is the Force Send action as a function: the Execution Queue's Force Send buttons (`useQueueHandlers`, which passes its own dispatcher) and Quick Chat (which queues a prompt for a tab it knows is free, then runs it) share it, so "safe to send right now" cannot mean two things. It refuses when the item's own tab is mid-turn (a tab runs one turn at a time), stamps `forceParallel` when ANOTHER tab of the agent is busy (that badges the chat entry and tells the on-exit dequeue guard this was deliberate), applies `applyQueuedItemDispatch`, and owns the dispatch rejection so it is never an unhandled promise; recovery from a failed dispatch stays in `agentStore.processQueuedItem`, the only place that knows WHY it failed. The default dispatcher reads its deps from `getDispatchDeps()` in `retryStore.ts` (registered by `useQueueProcessing`) and leaves the item queued when they are not there yet. Do NOT fire `maestro:remoteCommand` with `force: true` at a tab instead: it never queues, it gives back no id to follow, and against a busy tab it resets the tab to idle while the real turn is still running. `removeQueuedItem` returns whether the item was STILL THERE, deciding it inside one store update, so a caller that means to send the prompt somewhere else can trust `true` and must not resend on `false` (it already started). One trap: an item in the queue is not always WAITING. A turn the provider refused is parked back at the head of its tab's queue by Agent Resilience (or a context compaction) until the resend, and that recovery resends from its OWN snapshot, so removing the queue copy does not stop it and moving it elsewhere would run it twice. Ask `isHeldByRecovery(itemId)` in `src/renderer/stores/retryStore.ts` first, and end a withdrawn turn's recovery with `cancelRecoveryForTab(sessionId, tabId)`, which is scoped to that one tab. `enqueuePromptForTab({ expandCommands: true })` is for text a PERSON typed: a message that is exactly one of their slash commands is queued as that command, resolved against the same sets `processQueuedItem` searches. A queued item's card in the tab carries `LogEntry.queuedItemId` (messages via `markTabRunningQueuedItem`, commands via `addLogToTab`, which must keep the stamp); that id is how a caller follows its prompt from queued, to running, to the reply.
- **The tab a queued item is going to:** `resolveQueuedItemTabName(session, item)` in `src/renderer/utils/executionQueue.ts` for the LABEL, `resolveQueuedItemTarget()` in `src/renderer/utils/tabHelpers/index.ts` for the tab itself. Unlike `turnSettings`, a `QueuedItem`'s `tabName` is NOT frozen on purpose - it is a last-known fallback for a tab that is gone by the time the queue drains. Do NOT render `item.tabName` directly: an item queued into a brand-new tab snapshots `New` and keeps it forever, so after auto-naming the Execution Queue indicator grouped one tab under whichever name its FIRST item happened to carry, and the browser listed two rows for one tab under two different names. The resolver reads the live tab, then a closed-but-draining orphan, then the snapshot. See [UI-PATTERNS.md → Queued Item Tab Labels](UI-PATTERNS.md#queued-item-tab-labels-resolvequeueditemtabname).
- **Whether a queued item's tab is still mid-turn:** `isQueuedItemTargetBusy(session, item)` in `src/renderer/utils/tabHelpers/index.ts`. The agent process is keyed per TAB (`${sessionId}-ai-${tabId}`), so an agent can read idle, or a parallel `-batch-` Auto Run task can exit, while the exact tab an item would run on is still running its own turn; dispatching there makes main refuse the spawn (`Agent process already running for session ...-ai-<tabId>`, MAESTRO-ZS). It resolves the target through `resolveQueuedItemTarget()`, so the active-tab fallback and a closed-but-draining orphan are both covered - `getQueueBusyContext` keys off `item.tabId` alone and misses both. Every drain path asks it before dequeuing (`useQueueProcessing`, the Auto Run task exit in `useAgentExecution`, run completion in `useBatchHandlers`) and leaves a busy target's item queued: that tab's own exit drains it. A path that holds an item this way must keep the agent busy while the target runs, counting orphans (`getBusyTabs(session, { includeOrphans: true })`), or anything waiting on an idle agent, such as the Auto Run queue wait, moves on before the held item has run.
- **Editing the newest queued message:** `requestEditLastQueuedMessage()` in `src/renderer/services/editQueuedMessage.ts`. Two surfaces offer it (the `editLastQueuedMessage` chord and the palette's `Edit Last Queued Message`) and both route here, so they cannot drift on which item they pick, which tab they land on, or what they say when there is nothing to edit. Everything is read from the stores AT CALL TIME, never from a render snapshot - the pencil on a queued row reads live props, so a stale snapshot is the one way this can claim nothing is queued while a card sits on screen. A missing tab RANKS rather than filters (a closed tab must not turn into "nothing is queued"), only `type === 'command'` items are skipped, and the two empties are named apart: `Nothing queued to edit` vs `Only commands are queued`. See [RENDERER-SERVICES.md](RENDERER-SERVICES.md#editqueuedmessagets---edit-the-newest-queued-message).
- **Recovering a turn refused on the context limit:** `getContextCompactCommand()` in `src/shared/contextCompaction.ts`; `compactTabContext()`, `recordContextCompaction()`, `settleContextCompaction()` in `src/renderer/services/contextCompaction.ts`; driven by `scheduleRetryForError()` in `src/renderer/stores/retryStore.ts`, drawn by `<ContextCompactionCard>` from `LogEntry.contextCompaction`. The failure it replaced: a conversation that outgrew the model's context window had its next turn refused ("Prompt is too long"), and the user's message was lost behind an error whose only advice was to start a new session. The recovery compacts the provider session in place and resends the snapshotted prompt; the failure is shown nowhere and a pill marks where the conversation was cut down. Invariants: the compaction starts only from `noteFailedTurnExited()` (the exit listener), never from a timer, because it is a second process on the same provider session and because the exit listener reads an in-flight retry entry on exit as a resend that settled; one compaction per failed turn (an entry already present declines, which is also what stops the hand-back from looping); a compaction that cannot land hands the ORIGINAL error back through `registerAgentErrorSurfacer()` so it renders exactly as it always did, with no second copy of the error UI logic; output the failed turn prints after its error is dropped (`isAwaitingContextCompaction()`), since Claude prints the failure text on the result envelope as ordinary output; Stop ends it quietly through `cancelContextCompaction()`. The compaction runs through `spawnBackgroundSynopsis` with `{ keepTurnSettings, allowEmptyResponse }`: it prints nothing on success, and its summary becomes the conversation, so it must not run on the cheap synopsis tier. A provider is compacted only when it is listed in `getContextCompactCommand()`; add one after verifying its command works as a headless turn, never by assumption (Codex's `/compact` would reach the model as literal text). Not covered: Auto Run tasks (the batch owns that turn and its error).
- **Whether a Force Send control exists at all:** `shouldOfferForceSend(eligibility)` in `src/renderer/utils/executionQueue.ts`, paired with `getForceSendEligibility()` (can it run?) and `getForceSendTitle()` (why not?). Rendering and enabling are separate questions: `canForce` decides DISABLED, this decides PRESENT. Only `needs-forced-parallel` is worth showing dimmed, because its tooltip names a setting the user can go turn on; `no-target-tab` could never work, and `target-tab-busy` resolves itself (a tab runs one turn at a time, so the item is already next in line). Both surfaces - the inline QUEUED card in `QueuedItemsList` and the Execution Queue browser's Send Now - call it, so they cannot drift on when the button appears, which they had already done once in both directions.
- **Model/effort badges on a finished turn:** `<TurnSettingPills>` in `src/renderer/components/ui/TurnSettingPills.tsx`, fed from `LogEntry.turnModel` / `turnEffort`. Those are stamped at SEND time by `codifyTurnSettings()` in `src/renderer/utils/providerTabSessions.ts` - spread it into whatever tab patch marks the tab busy. Never label a turn from the live tab or agent value: settings are codified at send, so a model change made mid-stream must not relabel the response already running. **A QUEUED message freezes its settings when it is queued**, since the queue may not drain until several model changes later: every path that builds a `QueuedItem` spreads `captureQueuedTurnSettings()` into `item.turnSettings`, and both the pills (`markTabRunningQueuedItem`) and the spawn (`agentStore.processQueuedItem`) read it back through `codifyQueuedTurnSettings()`. The presence of the object is the capture flag - an undefined model INSIDE it means "the agent default was chosen", so never `?? ` it against the live value. **Both pills carry `data-turn-setting-pill` and leave every card on a phone**, hidden by one rule in the "Phone layout" block of `src/renderer/index.css`: the queued card's footer is a three-column grid whose `whitespace-nowrap` Force Send button and four-icon cluster cannot yield width, so at 390px the outer tracks overflowed into the centered pills and all three groups painted on top of each other. A readout with a `title` tooltip no touch screen can open is the thing to drop there, the same call the header's cost, context and LOCAL badges get; mark a new pill in this family with the attribute rather than gating it on `usePhoneLayout()` in JSX. See [UI-PATTERNS.md → Turn Attribution Pills](UI-PATTERNS.md#turn-attribution-pills-turnsettingpills).
- **Thinking mode (`'off' | 'on' | 'sticky'`):** `THINKING_MODES`, `nextThinkingMode()`, `asThinkingMode()` in `src/shared/types.ts`. One cycle order serves the composer's Thinking chip and `maestro-cli tab thinking <tab-id> cycle`, so a click and a CLI cycle cannot disagree about what comes next - do NOT hand-roll another `['off', 'on', 'sticky']` ladder or an inline `indexOf(...) + 1` step. Narrow anything arriving from the CLI or the web bridge through `asThinkingMode()` rather than casting: `'off'` is a truthy string, and an unrecognized mode written straight into a tab is a permanently wrong chip rather than a rejected command.
- **The red Stop pill:** `<StopTurnButton>` in `src/renderer/components/ui/StopTurnButton.tsx`. Stop is ONE agent-level action - it signals the agent's own process, every other busy tab it owns, and every cross-agent consult it fanned out - so every surface offering it draws the same control. Two surfaces do: the thinking pill (while the agent is working) and the cross-agent "N agents responding" pill (when only consulted agents are working, so the thinking pill is absent). They are mutually exclusive by construction in `InputArea` - the indicator is handed `onInterrupt` only when the thinking pill's own render condition is false - because both buttons run the same interrupt and two on screen is one control drawn twice. Do NOT hand-roll another red pill: a copy drifts on the square glyph, the padding, and worse, on what its tooltip claims the button stops.
- **Opening the expanded staged-image organizer:** `requestOpenStagedImagesOrganizer()` and `OPEN_STAGED_IMAGES_ORGANIZER_EVENT` in `src/renderer/services/stagedImagesOrganizer.ts`. The organizer's open state is LOCAL to `StagedImagesStrip`, which sits several levels below the app-level keyboard handler that owns the `openImageOrganizer` shortcut, so the request rides one app-level `CustomEvent` in the same shape as `requestHeadingPalette` and `requestTranscriptScrollToBottom` rather than a callback drilled up through `InputArea` and `MainPanel`. It is fire-and-forget on purpose: the strip only mounts while images are staged, so a keypress with an empty composer is a silent no-op instead of an empty modal. Both ends check `stagedImages.length > 1` - the handler so the shortcut is not recorded as a tracked event when there is nothing to organize, the strip because it is the one that knows the live count. One image is the lightbox's job (`Cmd+Y`), not the organizer's. Do NOT hand-roll another dispatch of this event name, and do NOT prop-drill the setter instead: exactly one strip is mounted at a time, which is what makes the event unambiguous.
- **The `src` of a conversation image:** `displayImageSrc(src)` in `src/renderer/utils/sessionImageSrc.ts`, over the reference grammar in `src/shared/sessionImageRefs.ts` (`SESSION_IMAGE_REF_PREFIX`, `sessionImageRefBasename()`, `sessionImageHttpPath()`). A persisted conversation image is a `maestro-image://store/<sha256>.<ext>` reference into the content-addressed store (`src/main/storage/session-image-store.ts`). The Electron app loads that scheme through the protocol handler in `src/main/index.ts`; a browser has no handler for it and renders a broken-image glyph, which is what every stored screenshot looked like on the web interface. In web-desktop the resolver rewrites the reference to the token-scoped `/<token>/api/images/<name>` route (`src/main/web-server/routes/imageRoutes.ts`); everywhere else, and for inline `data:` URLs, it passes the value through untouched. Every `<img>` that renders a transcript, queue, staged, group-chat, or wizard image goes through it (ten sites), so the decision is made once. The basename regex is also the traversal guard: only a lowercase-hex sha256 with a known image extension ever resolves, from the store, the route, or the resolver, and all three read the ONE regex in `shared/`. Do NOT re-declare the prefix or the grammar; do NOT resolve images to data URLs on the way out of the bridge instead (that is the 272MB sessions-file problem the store exists to solve, now on the wire). A transcript CHIP additionally wraps the result in `sessionImageThumbnailSrc(src, w, h)` (see **Referencing / thumbnailing a pasted transcript image**), and the order matters: `displayImageSrc` runs FIRST, because thumbnails ride the custom scheme and the thumbnail helper no-ops on anything that is not a bare ref, so the desktop gets a rendition while a browser client keeps receiving originals. Appending the query first leaves a `maestro-image://` URL no browser can load.
- **Referencing / thumbnailing a pasted transcript image:** `IMAGE_REF_PREFIX`, `isSessionImageRef()`, `sessionImageThumbnailSrc(ref, maxW, maxH)` in `src/shared/sessionImageRefs.ts`. Pasted images are relocated out of `maestro-sessions.json` into a content-addressed store and referenced as `maestro-image://store/<sha256>.<ext>` (see `src/main/storage/session-image-store.ts`). The prefix used to be a bare `'maestro-image://'` literal repeated across the store, `clipboard.ts`, `imageExport.ts`, and the transcript; it lives here so main and renderer share ONE definition and so the renderer can build a URL without importing fs-dependent main-process code. `sessionImageThumbnailSrc` appends the `?tw=&th=` query that the `maestro-image` protocol handler reads to serve a disk-cached downscaled rendition (`src/main/storage/session-image-thumbnails.ts`). Use it for ANY fixed-size chip: a transcript screenshot is routinely 4984x2578 / 13MB, and Chromium has no way to decode "just the small version" - it decodes the full 12-megapixel bitmap (~48MB RGBA, ~70ms) to paint a 200x80 chip. A field trace of one tab found 47 images totalling 193 megapixels (~0.7GB decoded) thrashing the decode cache on every scroll pass. A BARE ref (no query) must keep serving the original bytes - the lightbox, clipboard copy, and every export path depend on it - so `resolveToFilePath` strips the query rather than treating it as part of the basename, and the traversal guard still runs on the stripped basename. Pair it with `loading="lazy"` + `decoding="async"` on the `<img>`; the thumbnail is what makes the decode cheap, lazy is what stops off-screen work entirely.

## Cross-agent consults

- **Consulting another agent from a message (`@mention`):** `planCrossAgentMentions()` / `dispatchCrossAgentMentions()` / `dispatchCrossAgentMentionsForMessage()` in `src/renderer/services/crossAgentMentions.ts`, over `sendCrossAgentRequest()` in `src/renderer/hooks/agent/useCrossAgentDispatch.ts`. Resolution and dispatch are SEPARATE on purpose, because when a consult fires is part of the contract: a message sent while the agent is busy goes to the execution queue, so `useInputProcessing` plans at submit (it needs `suppressLocal` to know whether the source agent answers at all), stamps `crossAgentMention: true` on the `QueuedItem`, and `agentStore.processQueuedItem` fires the consult when the item becomes the agent's turn. **Never consult at submit time** - that pulls the other agent into a question still sitting several messages deep in the queue. This holds for a LEADING mention too (`suppressLocal`, where the source agent does not answer at all): it queues as a `crossAgentOnly` item, and `processQueuedItem` fires the consult and returns without spawning, releasing the dequeue's busy state via `applyQueuedItemRelease`. "This agent doesn't answer it" is not "it has nothing to wait for" - the item's queue POSITION is the user's instruction. Use `hasWorkAheadOfNewMessage()` (`utils/executionQueue.ts`) to make that call. Both entry points are module functions, not hook members, because the queue drain runs outside React. The CLI/web dispatch paths ride the SAME planner: `useRemoteHandlers` plans on the immediate path (a leading mention consults and skips the local spawn) and `useRemoteIntegration` stamps `crossAgentMention`/`crossAgentOnly` on a `dispatch --queue` item - so `maestro-cli dispatch "@Agent ..."` consults exactly like a typed message. Do NOT add a dispatch entry point that hands text straight to a spawn; a mention it never planned is silently inert. Anything that rewrites a queued item's text must re-derive BOTH flags from a fresh plan (`handleEditQueueItem`), never just `crossAgentMention`: where the mention sits is what decides whether this agent answers at all, and an edit can move it, so a stale `crossAgentOnly` silently discards half of what the user changed. And `suppressLocal` with no tab to anchor the reply on is a DROP, not a fallback - letting it through hands the local agent a message addressed to somebody else, which is the one thing the flag exists to prevent. On the main side, `cross-agent-router.ts` spawns the target with `target.customPath || agent.path || agent.command` (same rule as the tab spawn and the Group Chat moderator): detection probes known install dirs BEFORE PATH, so a consult that ignored the agent's `customPath` ran a stale nvm codex stub while the tab ran the real one. **ONLY AGENTS ARE TARGETS.** `resolveMentionedTargetSessionIds()` and `buildKnownMentionNameSet()` (`hooks/input/useAgentMentionCompletion.ts`) both skip groups: a group is picker shorthand that expands to its members' own `@name` tokens at accept time (`memberMentionValue`), so the sent text names agents and nothing else. Resolving a group here too is how an agent that merely SHARED a name with a group had its message fanned out to that whole group - suggestions are built groups-first, so the group won the name every time - and it would also let a message leading with an undispatchable token suppress the local send, addressing it to nobody. **Stop is agent-level and must reach the consults.** A mention fans one turn out across the agent's own tab plus one ephemeral `cross-agent-*` process per target, none of which carry the agent's process id, so `useInterruptHandler` calls `window.maestro.crossAgent.cancel(sessionId)` (non-critical - a failure there must not block the interrupt). Cancellation is addressed by SOURCE AGENT, never by request id: the renderer only learns a request id once `crossAgent.send` resolves, and main holds the authoritative registry (`cancelCrossAgentRequestsForSource`), registered before the target binary is resolved and re-checked around the spawn so a Stop landing in either race still lands. A cancelled consult settles like a timeout but stamped `canceled`, not `error` - the user stopping a consult is not the target failing to answer, and `crossAgentTerminationNote()` is the single place that wording is chosen. See [RENDERER-SERVICES.md](RENDERER-SERVICES.md#crossagentmentionsts-115-lines).
- **Holding a mentioning turn open until its consult replies:** `holdTurnForConsults()`, `settleConsultHold()`, `withConsultPendingNote()`, `interruptConsultHolds()` in `src/renderer/services/crossAgentConsultHold.ts`, over the queue barrier `hasConsultHoldForTab()` / `dropConsultHold()` in `src/renderer/utils/executionQueue.ts`. A mid-message `@mention` (`!suppressLocal`) is answered by BOTH agents, and the two used to run blind: the local turn never learned a consult was running, finished alone (once deciding the mentioned agent did not exist and redoing its work by hand), and the reply landed in the transcript where the local agent never read it. Now `dispatchCrossAgentMentions()` puts an `awaitingConsult` item at the HEAD of the queue and returns the targets; every spawn path appends the `cross-agent-consult-pending` note to the local prompt (work, do not finish); each terminal chunk settles the hold by (source agent, source tab, target agent) - never by request id, so it survives a web-desktop reload - and the last reply releases it into a continuation whose `agentContext` carries the replies verbatim inside `cross-agent-consult-reply`. Four rules: the hold blocks ITS TAB only (`firstRunnableQueueIndex`), so other tabs keep draining while nothing new for that tab can slip between the turn and its conclusion, forced parallel included; `agentContext` is prepended at spawn and never rendered, because the replies already sit in the transcript as the consult's own bubble; a CANCELED consult removes the hold (Stop means stop) while a FAILED one is recorded as a reply so the agent can still finish; and a turn whose spawn fails drops its hold (`applyQueuedItemDispatchFailure`, the composer catch), or a later continuation would answer a message the agent never received. A cold desktop start settles every pending hold as interrupted, since no consult process survives a restart. Do NOT spawn the continuation directly or settle on `onComplete` alone: the queue is what orders it, and a closure is what a reload loses.
- **When a mention runs (parallel / consult first / hand-off):** `inferMentionTiming()` in `src/shared/crossAgentContext.ts`, carried as `CrossAgentMentionPlan.routing`; `armMentionHandoff()`, `releaseMentionHandoff()`, `dropMentionHandoffs()` in `src/renderer/services/crossAgentHandoff.ts`. The order comes from the user's WORDING ("check with @X first", "then send what you find to @X"), so a typed message, a queued edit, and `maestro-cli dispatch` route the same way. `MentionRoutingBar` shows the decision before send, and its mode menu lets the composer user override it for one message: `planCrossAgentMentions(message, id, routingOverride)` applies the pick (`routingSource: 'explicit'`), the override lives in `composerInputStore.mentionRoutingOverride`, and a queued message carries it as `QueuedItem.mentionRouting` via `queuedMentionFields()`, because the drain re-plans from the text. Mode names, summaries and example phrasings live once in `mentionRoutingCopy.ts`. The CLI reaches the same override with `dispatch --routing <mode>`: every boundary reads it with `parseMentionRoutingField()` (over `isMentionRouting()` and `MENTION_ROUTINGS`, the menu's order), which refuses an unknown string instead of dropping it, and the field rides `send_command` / `enqueue_command` / `new_ai_tab_with_prompt` into `planCrossAgentMentions()` or `buildQueuedMessageItem({ mentionRouting })`, never a side channel. Consult-first is a consult hold with `deferred` set (no local spawn; the release IS the answering turn), and a hand-off is `AITab.pendingMentionHandoff`, released by the exit listener on a clean exit and cleared by Stop. Build the local turn's prompt with `withMentionTurnNotes()`, not `withConsultPendingNote()` alone, or a hand-off turn never learns its answer is forwarded. Write access is per kind: `resolveCrossAgentWritable()` (`src/main/ipc/handlers/cross-agent.ts`) reads `crossAgentHandoffsWritable` (default ON) for a hand-off and `crossAgentMentionsWritable` (default off) for a consult, and a hand-off's prompt uses `handoffGrant()`, never the consult's `cwdGrant()`.
- **An agent asking another agent (`maestro-cli ask`):** `runCrossAgentAsk()` in `src/renderer/services/crossAgentAsk.ts`, over the same `sendCrossAgentRequest()` a typed `@mention` uses. `dispatch` is NOT the verb for a question: it writes into the target's ACTIVE tab, so the question lands mid-conversation in whatever the human has open with that agent, and the answer goes to the screen instead of back to the agent that asked. `--background` does not fix that - it decides where the VIEW lands, not which conversation the prompt joins, so a backgrounded dispatch interrupts QUIETLY, which is worse. The path is `cross_agent_ask` (WS) -> `handleCrossAgentAsk` -> the `consultAgent` callback -> `remote:crossAgentAsk` -> `runCrossAgentAsk`, and the ONLY thing it adds over the mention path is `SendCrossAgentRequestOptions.onComplete`, which hands the finished text to a caller blocked on it. Three rules the resolver encodes: the consult is keyed on the CALLING AGENT plus the constant `CROSS_AGENT_ASK_TAB_ID`, never the caller's active tab, so one consult tab per (caller -> target) pairing survives the agent switching tabs and a follow-up `ask` resumes the earlier thread; **no transcript is forwarded by default** (`ask` sends a self-contained question - `--with-context` opts in, and `buildCrossAgentPrompt` swaps to a header that does not announce a transcript that is not there); and it RESOLVES rather than rejects on every outcome including a missing target, because the caller is a CLI process reporting to an agent and an exception there reads as a broken command instead of "that agent could not answer". `onComplete` is guaranteed to fire exactly once, which is why `completedRequests` remembers the COMPLETION rather than just the id: `crossAgent.send` resolves on a round trip to main and "target agent not found" is emitted before it does, so a bare id set let the `.then()` return having never delivered, and the CLI sat there until its own timeout for a consult that failed instantly. The main-side wait is the caller's timeout, not the dispatch path's 3s delivery receipt - here we wait for an ANSWER, not for the renderer to accept a prompt. See [CLI-UI-PARITY.md](CLI-UI-PARITY.md#ask-is-not-dispatch---background).
- **Marking an agent's own hand-off in its transcript (`dispatch` / `ask` from its shell):** `readCallerIdentity()`, `buildCallerIdentityEnv()`, `callerMessageFields()`, `readCallerMessageFields()`, `isSelfDispatch()` in `src/shared/agentDelegation.ts`; `noteDispatchDelegation()` in `src/main/web-server/handlers/messageHandlers/agentDelegation.ts`; `recordAgentDelegation()` / `settleAgentDelegation()` in `src/renderer/services/agentDelegation.ts`, drawn by `<AgentDelegationCard>` from `LogEntry.delegation`. The failure it replaced: a typed `@mention` shows who was consulted through its reply's attribution header, but an agent that ran `maestro-cli dispatch` or `ask` itself left only a command line inside a tool card. A mention that does not resolve to a known agent is plain text, so the source agent delegates by hand and the same intent was visible one way and buried the other. Invariants: every agent spawn stamps `MAESTRO_CALLER_AGENT_ID` / `MAESTRO_CALLER_TAB_ID` into `effectiveCustomEnvVars` in `handle-spawn.ts` (the session layer, so it survives `STRIPPED_ENV_VARS`, which kills a stale copy inherited from whatever launched Maestro); the CLI forwards them as `fromSessionId` / `fromTabId`, the same wire names `cross_agent_ask` already used; a dispatch pill is recorded only AFTER the renderer accepted the prompt, routed to the CALLER's window; an `ask` records its own pill in `runCrossAgentAsk` as `pending` and settles it when the consult ends, because only the renderer learns the outcome. Traps: do NOT reuse `MAESTRO_AGENT_ID`, which tells `maestro-cli pianola watch` it IS Pianola and switches on its judgment handoff; a named source tab that has closed gets NO pill rather than a guessed tab (the fallback to a lone busy tab, then the active tab, is only for a shell with no tab stamp); and a dispatch into the caller's own tab (or its agent's active tab, with no tab named) is a loop, not a hand-off, so `isSelfDispatch` drops it. The entry is a self-contained card in `isSelfContainedCard`, so streamed output after it starts a new entry instead of being glued onto the pill. CLI tests that assert a message payload must call `isolateAgentEnv([CALLER_AGENT_ID_ENV_VAR, CALLER_TAB_ID_ENV_VAR])`, or they fail when run from inside an agent shell.
- **The consultation budget setting (peer consults and delegations per user message):** `MAX_CONSULTATIONS_PER_TURN_SETTING`, `DEFAULT_MAX_CONSULTATIONS_PER_TURN`, `MAX_CONSULTATIONS_PER_TURN_CEILING`, `normalizeMaxConsultationsPerTurn(raw)` in `src/shared/consultBudget.ts`. One number (`maxConsultationsPerTurn`, default 25) bounds every consult (`maestro-cli ask`, an agent's `@mention`) and every delegation (`maestro-cli dispatch`) in the WHOLE tree that descends from one user message, so it caps fan-out and chain length together where a depth cap would cap only one. A peer roster plus a group grant makes a five-agent group a fully connected consult graph that nobody typed, which is why the bound exists. Three processes read the value (the renderer's setter, main, and the CLI), and `maestro-cli settings set` stores any JSON under the key with no range check, so EVERY reader goes through `normalizeMaxConsultationsPerTurn()`: never `settingsStore.get('maxConsultationsPerTurn', 25)` and never `readSettingValue(...) || 25`. The trap is `0`: it is a real value (peer consults and delegations off), so `|| default` silently turns a deliberate off back into 25. A missing or non-numeric value is the default, a fraction rounds DOWN (a budget is never more than was typed), and there is no "unlimited": the ceiling is a safety bound and a tree that size is already a runaway. The Settings control (`ConsultationBudgetSection` in `Settings/tabs/GeneralTab/components/`) keeps a draft string for the same reason: an emptied field must not commit `0` while the user is typing a new number.
- **Spending a consult chain's budget (the shared counter):** `spendConsultBudget(chainId, limit, options?)`, `readConsultBudgetSpent(chainId, options?)`, `sweepExpiredChains(options?)`, `newConsultChainId()`, `CONSULT_BUDGET_FILE` and `CONSULT_CHAIN_TTL_MS` in `src/main/peer-consults/consult-budget.ts`, with the id check `isConsultChainId()` in `src/shared/consultBudget.ts`. A chain is every consult and delegation that descends from one user message, and its members are separate processes (an agent's shell running `maestro-cli ask`, the peer's shell running `maestro-cli dispatch`, the app spawning an `@mention` consult), so the count lives in ONE file, `consult-budget.json` in Maestro's data directory, shaped `{ "<chainId>": { count, updatedAt } }`. A file rather than app state because a headless CLI with the app closed must still be bounded. The implementation is in `src/shared/maestro-lib/agents/consult-budget.ts`; the main-process path re-exports it for existing callers. It imports no Electron, so the desktop, CLI and detached runtime share the same counter. `spendConsultBudget` is one read-modify-write under `withDirectoryLock`, written back with `atomicWriteJsonSync`: it returns `{ allowed, spent }`, a refused spend does NOT count (so `spent` on a refusal is the true total and a raised setting takes effect on the next attempt), and a refusal still refreshes the chain's `updatedAt` so a runaway cannot wait out the expiry and start over. Pass `limit` through `normalizeMaxConsultationsPerTurn()`; `0` refuses everything. Every write also drops chains idle longer than `CONSULT_CHAIN_TTL_MS` (6 hours), which is what stops a crashed run from leaking an entry forever. **It fails closed.** A counter that is corrupt, unreadable, locked past the timeout, or handed an id that fails `isConsultChainId()` refuses the consult and returns `problem`, a sentence naming the file; it never throws and it NEVER rewrites a damaged file as an empty one, because a recovery that resets the count is a way around the budget for exactly the runaway it exists to stop. A caller must show `problem` when it is set (it is the only report of a broken counter) and must not read `spent: 0` beside it as a number. `readConsultBudgetSpent` is the read without a spend, for showing the remaining budget: `null` means not known, so leave the sentence out rather than print a guess. Tests MUST pass `{ dataDir }` pointing at a temp directory: `MAESTRO_USER_DATA` is set in an agent's shell, so the default is the live data directory even with Electron mocked. Do not keep a second count anywhere (renderer state, app memory): a count one process cannot see is a budget that process does not spend.
- **Which consult chain a spawned agent is in (`MAESTRO_CONSULT_CHAIN` / `MAESTRO_CONSULT_DEPTH`):** `ConsultChain` (`{ chainId, depth }`), `CONSULT_CHAIN_ENV_VAR`, `CONSULT_DEPTH_ENV_VAR`, `readConsultChainEnv(env)`, `buildConsultChainEnv(chain)`, `childConsultChain(chain)`, `parseConsultChain(value)` and `withoutConsultChainEnv(env)` in `src/shared/consultBudget.ts`. The members of a chain are separate processes (an agent's shell runs `maestro-cli ask`, which spawns the peer, whose own shell runs `maestro-cli dispatch`), so the only thing that tells the last of them it descends from the first one's turn is its environment. The chain id is the key the counter spends on; the depth (0 at the root) is a description for refusals and logs, NOT the bound. **Who states it.** The desktop spawn (`src/main/ipc/handlers/process/handle-spawn.ts`) puts both variables in `effectiveCustomEnvVars`, beside the caller identity, so they reach the local and the SSH env-merge paths. A spawn whose request carries `consultChain` (`SpawnProcessConfig`, the chain the process runs IN, already at its own depth) is a descendant and keeps it, whatever the agent's own peer configuration: an agent with no roster in the middle of a chain must not hand what it starts a fresh budget. A spawn with none is the root of a user message and mints one at depth 0 with `newConsultChainId()`, but only when `hasPeerGrant()` says the agent has a roster, so most agents carry no new environment. A terminal is never in a chain. **The app never hands on a chain it inherited.** Both names are in `STRIPPED_ENV_VARS` (`src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/env.ts`, the desktop surface), deleted in `buildPtyTerminalEnv()`, and dropped by the Cue surface (`buildCueAgentEnvironment`, the one exception to "Cue inherits `process.env` as it is"): Maestro launched from inside an agent turn (the normal case in development) would otherwise put every agent it spawns in that one stale chain and exhaust its budget for all of them. So a spawn in the app is in a chain only when the spawner says so through the env record it passes (`buildConsultChainEnv(...)` in the session layer or `maestroEnvVars`). **The CLI surface is the opposite, and that is correct:** `buildCliAgentEnvironment` inherits the shell's environment whole, so an agent that `maestro-cli` spawns from inside an agent's shell starts with the CALLER's chain at the caller's depth. A verb that spawns a peer (`send`, `ask`) must therefore state the child's own place, `buildConsultChainEnv(childConsultChain(mine))`, through `maestroEnvVars`, which is applied over the inherited copy. Rules for a reader: read with `readConsultChainEnv(process.env)` or `parseConsultChain(message.consultChain)`, never `process.env.MAESTRO_CONSULT_CHAIN` directly, because both check the id with `isConsultChainId()` (an id that fails is no chain, and the counter would refuse it anyway). A chain id with a missing or malformed depth is still that chain at depth 0: dropping it would reset the budget. Anything that keys a cache on how an agent is CONFIGURED must drop the chain with `withoutConsultChainEnv()` next to `withoutCallerIdentityEnv()` (the omp model catalog key does), since a root spawn's chain id is new on every turn.
- **Admitting a consult or delegation (spend, refuse, place the target):** `admitConsult(request, options?)` in `src/main/peer-consults/consult-budget.ts`; `admitCliConsult(env?, options?)` in `src/cli/services/consult-budget-guard.ts`; `describeConsultBudgetRefusal()`, `CONSULT_BUDGET_EXHAUSTED_CODE`, `CONSULT_BUDGET_UNAVAILABLE_CODE` in `src/shared/consultBudget.ts`; `SpawnAgentOptions.consultChain` in `src/cli/services/agent-spawner.ts`; `QueuedItem.consultChain`, `ProcessConfig.consultChain` in `src/renderer/types/index.ts`; `CrossAgentRequest.consultChain` in `src/shared/crossAgentTypes.ts`. `spendConsultBudget()` only counts. The RULE for one consult is `admitConsult()`: who spends, who passes free, and which place in the chain the target takes. Every verb that reaches another agent calls it once, after its arguments are known to be good and before anything is spawned or sent, so a mixed chain (`send`, `ask`, `dispatch`, an agent's `@mention`) is counted the same way at every hop. A CLI verb calls `admitCliConsult()`, which supplies the three inputs from its process: the chain its shell inherited (`readConsultChainEnv`), whether an agent's shell ran it (`readCallerIdentity`), and the setting through `normalizeMaxConsultationsPerTurn(readSettingValue(...))`. Three callers, and the asymmetry is deliberate. (1) A caller IN a chain spends one from it and its target sits one hop down (`childConsultChain`). (2) An AGENT in no chain (no peer roster, so `handle-spawn` minted nothing; or a Cue run) becomes the root of a new chain and spends the first of it, so a budget of `0` still refuses it. (3) The USER in no chain (a shell, a script, a typed `@mention`) is always let through and spends nothing, even at `0`: that call IS the user's message, not a consult made while working on one. It still mints a chain with the target at depth 0, because without one nothing the target starts would ever be counted. Do not "fix" case 3 by spending: a bot or a script that calls `maestro-cli send` would stop working the moment the user sets the budget to `0`. On success, hand `admission.chain` to whatever SPAWNS the target, as it is (do not add one again). There are two routes and both need it: a spawn this process makes takes `SpawnAgentOptions.consultChain`, which states the chain in the agent's environment over the inherited one and carries it to an SSH remote (which inherits nothing); a spawn the DESKTOP makes for the CLI takes the chain in the message (`plugins_send_agent.consultChain`, narrowed with `parseConsultChain` and passed through `HeadlessTurnSettings.consultChain`), because the CLI's own environment never reaches a process the app starts. Forget the second route and the budget silently stops at the first hop whenever the app is open. `dispatch` and `ask` only have the desktop route, and the chain has to survive every layer between the WebSocket message and the spawn: each message (`send_command`, `new_ai_tab_with_prompt`, `enqueue_command`, `cross_agent_ask`) carries `consultChain`; the handler narrows it with `parseConsultChain` and passes it down the callback; the renderer puts it on the `maestro:remoteCommand` event or on the queue item (`QueuedItem.consultChain`, set through `buildQueuedMessageItem()` / `enqueuePromptForTab()`), and the spawn sends it as `ProcessConfig.consultChain`, which `handle-spawn` states in the environment. An `ask` goes to the consult router instead: `CrossAgentRequest.consultChain` means the consulted agent's place, ALREADY admitted, so the router states it in the consult's environment and does not spend again. A `@mention` is admitted BY the router (`startCrossAgentRequest()` in `src/main/cross-agent/cross-agent-router.ts`), last, with the target resolved, through the same `admitConsult()`; the `cross-agent:send` handler hands it the setting as `maxConsultationsPerTurn`. Which case a mention is depends on one field, `CrossAgentRequest.sourceConsultChain`: the place of the turn that MADE the mention. A mention the user typed has none, so it is case 3: free, and the root of a new chain (one per mentioned agent). A mention inside a prompt another agent sent with `dispatch` has one, so it is case 1 and spends. The renderer never decides this and never mints: `dispatchCrossAgentMentions()` takes the turn's chain as its last argument (the queue drain passes `item.consultChain`, the remote path the event's `consultChain`) and puts it on every consult, on the armed hand-off (`MentionHandoff.sourceConsultChain`, spent when the answer goes out) and on the consult hold (`holdTurnForConsults(..., { consultChain })`), because a hold releases into a NEW spawn that would otherwise root a fresh chain. Never send a mention's chain as `consultChain`: that field says "already paid" and the router would not charge it. A refused mention is a terminal error chunk carrying the refusal text as it is, so the source agent's bubble and its consult hold both say why. A router test passes `consultBudgetStore: { dataDir }`. On `remote:executeCommand` the chain sits at a fixed position BEFORE the receipt channel, so the sender passes it even when it is undefined. The spend happens before the desktop is reached, so a dispatch the desktop then refuses (a busy tab) has still spent one. One caller opts out by name: `DispatchOptions.skipConsultBudget`, set only by Pianola's supervisor loops (`pianola watch`, `pianola orchestrate`), which dispatch for hours under their own rules and would stop at the 25th dispatch under a per-message budget. It is not a CLI flag, so an agent cannot ask for it; do not set it anywhere else. On a refusal, print `message` and `code` in the verb's own error shape and exit non-zero. Never write the text yourself: `describeConsultBudgetRefusal()` is the ONE wording for every verb, it names the budget, the number spent and the setting, and it prints no count when the counter itself was unusable (`problem`). A refusal that read like an empty answer would teach the calling agent that the peer had nothing to say. Tests of a verb mock `consult-budget-guard`; tests of the guard pass a temp `dataDir` and an explicit `env`, because the test process is itself an agent's shell.
- **Telling an agent what is left of its consult budget (the Peer Agents sentence):** `readConsultBudgetStatus({ chain?, limit }, options?)` in `src/main/peer-consults/consult-budget.ts`, the shape `ConsultBudgetStatus` (`{ limit, spent }`) and `remainingConsultBudget(status)` in `src/shared/consultBudget.ts`, and the sentence itself, `PeerPromptOptions.budget` on `formatPeerAgentsForPrompt()` in `src/shared/peerAgents.ts`. An agent with a peer roster reads ONE sentence in its `## Peer Agents` block: how many consults and delegations are left for this message, that both count, and that the peers it reaches draw on the same budget. It is there so the agent rations instead of finding the ceiling by being refused. The figure is a snapshot taken when the turn was spawned, and the sentence says so. `readConsultBudgetStatus` holds the one rule about which figure a turn gets: with a `chain` (the place the turn takes in a chain that exists: a consult, a `send`, a delegated prompt) it reads what that chain has spent; with no chain the turn is a ROOT whose chain is minted at the spawn, so nothing is spent. Each system prompt builder passes it on through `TemplateContext.consultBudget`: the main builder (`buildMaestroSystemPromptForSession(id, { consultChain })`, called by the `@mention` router with the chain the consult was just admitted to), the CLI builder (`prepareMaestroSystemPromptCli(agent, { consultChain })`, called by `send` and by the plugin headless runner), and the renderer builder (`prepareMaestroSystemPrompt({ consultChain })`), which cannot read the counter file or see the chain `process:spawn` is about to mint and so asks main over `window.maestro.crossAgent.consultBudget(consultChain?)` (`cross-agent:consultBudget`). **A builder that does not know which chain its spawn is in passes nothing, and the sentence is left out.** That is a Cue run, a Group Chat participant, and a CLI playbook or goal run today: their spawns mint no chain, so a figure would describe a budget the agent is not spending. Do not default those to "nothing spent". The sentence is also left out when the count cannot be read (`undefined` from the read, `null` over IPC) and when the agent has no peers, in which case no builder asks at all. Never print a placeholder in its place, and never compute the remainder by hand: `remainingConsultBudget()` clamps at zero (a budget lowered mid-run can sit under what was spent) and returns `undefined` for a figure that is not a count. A budget of `0` and a spent budget each get their own wording, which tells the agent to reach no peer on this turn. Tests pass `consultBudgetStore: { dataDir }` to the main and CLI builders for the same reason every counter test does.
- **The fleet's peer relationships as one directed graph (the Delegation Graph):** `buildDelegationGraph(scope, sessions, { placedIds? })`, the queries `edgeBetween(model, sourceId, targetId)`, `isMutual(model, a, b)`, `connectedAgentIds(model)`, `agentsOffGraph(model, sessions)` and `delegationGraphStats(model)`, the two text views `delegationAdjacency(model)` and `describeDelegationGraphStats(stats)`, the id rule `delegationEdgeId(sourceId, targetId)`, and the types `DelegationGraphModel`, `DelegationGraphNode`, `DelegationGraphEdge`, `DelegationGraphScope`, `DelegationGraphStats` in `src/shared/delegationGraph.ts`. The second view over the peer data: `resolvePeerRoster()` answers "who may THIS agent reach", and this assembles those answers for every agent into nodes and directed edges, so a canvas or a CLI verb can show the whole team. It owns NO persistence and NO precedence. An edge is a `ResolvedPeer` seen from the outside, so the rules about explicit rows, the group grant, the worktree grant and exclusions stay in `resolvePeerRoster()`; a graph that merged access pairs or checked `hasAnyPeerAccess()` itself would be a second copy of those rules, and the list and the graph would then disagree about the same agent. For the same reason there is no self-edge guard and no dangling-edge guard here: the roster has already dropped the agent itself, a row whose agent is gone, and a peer with neither bit. The edge model: ONE edge per ordered pair, carrying both bits (`read` = consult through `maestro-cli ask`, `write` = delegate through `maestro-cli dispatch`); the reverse relationship is a SEPARATE edge, so "bidirectional" is two edges existing and never a flag on one. Scope is `{ kind: 'all' }` or `{ kind: 'group', groupId }`, and a group scope matches on `groupId` equality the way the group grant does (a nested group's members are not in scope). An edge is kept when EITHER end is in scope, which is what makes a delegation that leaves the group and one that reaches into it both visible; the agent at the far end gets a node marked `scope: 'spill'`. An in-scope agent with no edges still gets a node, because an agent nobody can reach is the thing the graph exists to show. `placedIds` is the one other way onto a graph: an agent the user put on the canvas by hand gets a `spill` node with no edge, so there is something to draw an edge to. Placing is not moving (the node keeps the agent's own `groupId`) and it writes nothing; an id that is already on the graph, or that names no live agent, changes nothing, so a placed agent that is later connected is the same model with and without the placement. `agentsOffGraph()` is the complement: every live agent with no node, sorted like the nodes, which is what an "Add agent" control lists (always empty on the `all` scope). It takes `PeerRosterInput[]`, so the renderer passes its `Session`s and the CLI its own session records; do not map sessions to a new shape for it. Output is sorted (nodes by name then id, edges by id), so callers and tests never sort. Ask the model through the queries, never with your own loop over `model.edges`: `edgeBetween()` is directional (the edge back is a different edge), `isMutual()` is true when an edge exists in EACH direction whichever bits each carries (it walks the edge list, so a caller asking for every edge builds one `Set` of ids and tests `delegationEdgeId(target, source)`), `connectedAgentIds()` is every agent an edge touches at either end (an in-scope agent that is not in it is isolated, and a `spill` agent that is not in it was placed and is not connected yet; the canvas node reads its state through `agentDelegationNodeState(node, connectedIds)` in `src/renderer/components/DelegationGraph/nodes/AgentDelegationNode.tsx`, so the count and the marker cannot disagree), and `delegationGraphStats()` returns `{ agents, edges, mutual, isolated, spill, placed }` for a legend or an empty state. In the stats, `agents` includes the `spill` agents (in-scope is `agents - spill`), a mutual pair counts once in `mutual` and twice in `edges`, `isolated` is always in-scope agents, and `placed` is the `spill` agents no edge touches (a subset of `spill`, never counted as isolated: "nobody reaches it" is a fact about the fleet, "not connected yet" is a fact about one canvas). The two text views are for a surface with no canvas: `describeDelegationGraphStats(stats)` puts the counts in words (one line per fact, a zero count has no line), and the legend and `maestro-cli delegation graph` (`src/cli/commands/delegation-graph.ts`, printed by `formatDelegationGraph()` in `src/cli/output/formatter.ts`) both print it, so do NOT count a graph in other words; `delegationAdjacency(model)` is the model read by agent and not by edge (each node with `reaches` and `reachedBy`, every link carrying the peer node and whether the reverse edge exists). It holds no rule and is never an input to a write. The CLI verb is the READ half of the graph and needs no running app; it has no drawing mode, because `update-agent --peer` / `--remove-peer` already write every edge.
- **Where an agent sits on the Delegation Graph (saved per scope):** `useDelegationGraphLayout(scope)` in `src/renderer/components/DelegationGraph/useDelegationGraphLayout.ts` is what a canvas calls; the record is `delegationGraphLayouts` in `src/renderer/stores/settingsDelegationGraphSlice.ts` (`setDelegationScopeLayout()`, `resetDelegationScopeLayout()`, `sanitizeDelegationGraphLayouts()`, types `DelegationScopeLayout` and `DelegationGraphLayouts`); the storage rules are `scopeStorageKey()`, `parseScopeStorageKey()`, `parseSavedPositions()`, `mergeSavedPositions()`, `prunePositions()` and `DELEGATION_GRAPH_LAYOUTS_SETTING` in `src/shared/delegationGraphLayout.ts`. This is the PERSISTED position store. `saveNodePositions()` / `restoreNodePositions()` in `DocumentGraph/layoutAlgorithms.ts` look like one and are not: they write a module-level `Map` that dies with the renderer, so a graph built on them keeps its arrangement all through development and loses it on every quit. Positions are stored PER SCOPE (`'all'`, or `group:<groupId>`), because one agent sits in the fleet graph and in its group's graph at different places; never read one scope's coordinates in another, and never build the key by hand. Three modules on purpose: the layout vocabulary stays in the zero-import leaf `delegationLayoutTypes.ts`, the position rules are pure and shared, and the slice joins them into `{ layout, positions }`. The hook owns WHEN a change is written, which the store cannot know. `saveDraggedPositions()` is debounced through `useDebouncedCallback()` (a drag fires per mouse move, and each write sends the whole record through IPC), except the FIRST move of a hand arrangement, which is written at once so the scope reads `manual` while the node is still being dragged; pass it every node on the canvas, since that first write is what turns an algorithm's arrangement into a saved one. `saveAlgorithmLayout()` and `resetLayout()` write at once and CANCEL a waiting drag write, which would otherwise land afterwards and put the scope back on `manual`. `saveDroppedPositions()` is for an agent dropped on the canvas at a point: hand placement like a drag (the scope becomes `manual`, pass every node on the canvas plus the dropped one), and written AT ONCE even on a scope that is already `manual`, because the canvas reads the position back in the render that first draws the agent and a debounced write would draw it somewhere else first. `savePlacedPositions()` ADDS agents the canvas placed beside their neighbors: it keeps the layout, never replaces a saved position, writes nothing for a scope with no entry (that scope is arranged afresh on every open), and writes nothing when nothing is new, because the canvas calls it from an effect that re-runs on every save. `hasSavedLayout` says whether a reset has anything to forget. A waiting write carries the scope it was made for and is flushed on scope change and on unmount, so switching scope or closing the graph inside the debounce window loses nothing and writes to the right scope. The record is sparse and self-normalizing: `manual` with no positions is not stored (nothing was placed, so the next open would treat every agent as new and pile them up), the default layout with no positions is not stored (it is what a missing entry means), a layout this build cannot draw falls back to the default and KEEPS its positions, and a coordinate that is not a pair of finite numbers is dropped before it reaches disk (a diverged force layout yields `NaN`, which JSON writes as `null`). `mergeSavedPositions(nodes, saved)` returns `{ placed, unplaced }` and never passes an unplaced node off as placed; `arrangeDelegationGraph()` (next entry) is the one caller that turns the two lists into a full arrangement, so a new agent lands beside its neighbors instead of at the origin. `prunePositions(saved, liveSessionIds)` takes every agent that EXISTS, not every agent on the canvas, so a `spill` agent whose edge was removed keeps its place; it returns `saved` itself when nothing was dropped.
- **The Delegation Graph's layout names and edge line:** `DelegationLayoutType`, `DELEGATION_LAYOUT_TYPES`, `DELEGATION_LAYOUT_LABELS`, `DEFAULT_DELEGATION_LAYOUT`, `nextDelegationLayout()`, `isDelegationLayoutType()`, `isDelegationAlgorithmLayout()` in `src/renderer/components/DelegationGraph/delegationLayoutTypes.ts`, and `graphEdgeGeometry()`, `anchorOfNode()` in `src/renderer/utils/graphEdgeGeometry.ts`. Both graph canvases share this router. The layout vocabulary imports only the Document Graph's leaf labels and types, without loading d3-force; the global default is `DEFAULT_GLOBAL_DELEGATION_LAYOUT` (`lobes`), while groups and connected agent clusters default to `DEFAULT_DELEGATION_LAYOUT` (`hierarchical`). The toolbar picker, the cycle shortcut and the settings validator share one union and cannot disagree about which layouts exist. An edge runs between the two node BODIES, aimed center to center and stopped at each border, so it is the short way in every layout; the two directions of a mutual pair are two edges that each bow to their own side (`REVERSE_PAIR_SHIFT`, `REVERSE_PAIR_BOW`), because they can differ. Do NOT pin an edge to a top or bottom handle.
- **Drawing and arranging the Delegation Graph:** `<DelegationGraphModal theme onClose scope?>` in `src/renderer/components/DelegationGraph/DelegationGraphModal.tsx` is the window, opened with `getModalActions().openDelegationGraph(groupId?)` in `src/renderer/stores/modalStore.ts`, the one opener that takes a scope (surface `delegation-graph` in `UI_SURFACES`, shortcut id `openDelegationGraph`, which ships unbound). No group id opens it on every agent (the hamburger menu, the hotkey, the first palette command); a group id opens it on that group, from three places that all call the same action: "Delegation Graph" in the group's right-click menu (`onOpenDelegationGraph` on `GroupContextMenu`, offered on a worktree group too), one palette command per group (`buildDelegationGraphCommands({ groups })` in `QuickActionsModal/commands/delegationGraphCommands.ts`, ids from `delegationGraphGroupCommandId()`), and `maestro-cli open delegation-graph --group <id>`. `delegationScopeGroups(groups)` in `DelegationGraphPlacement.tsx` is the one order a list of those groups is drawn in (the scope select and the palette), and `delegationGroupLabel(group)` beside it is the one label; `<DelegationGraphView theme initialScope?>` in `src/renderer/components/DelegationGraph/DelegationGraphView.tsx` is the canvas (toolbar, legend, minimap, selected-agent panel); `useDelegationGraphModel(scope, placedIds?)` in `useDelegationGraphModel.ts` is the model it draws, and `useAgentsOffDelegationGraph(model)` beside it is the agents it does not; `arrangeDelegationGraph(model, layout, saved)` and `computeDelegationLayout(layout, model, { centerId? })` in `delegationLayouts.ts` say where each agent goes. The view has two modes, `DelegationGraphMode` (`'view' | 'edit'`), switched from its toolbar. It opens in View on EVERY open (`DEFAULT_DELEGATION_GRAPH_MODE`; the mode is plain state and is never persisted, so the graph cannot open already armed). In View it edits no roster: `nodesConnectable` is false, which is also what hides each node's handles (reactflow hands the node `isConnectable`), and the one way from it to a relationship is `openAgentSettings(session, { tab: 'peers' })`. Any gesture that writes a roster must be gated on Edit; a slipped drag on a dense graph must never rewrite an agent's permissions. In Edit an edge dragged from one agent to another opens `<DelegationEdgeEditor>` (`DelegationEdgeEditor.tsx`) where it was dropped: `onConnect` carries the pair and no pointer, `onConnectEnd` (a drag) or `onClickConnectEnd` (two clicks) carries the pointer, and reactflow calls them in that order, so the canvas opens the editor in the first and moves it in the second. A drop is a question and not a change: nothing is written before the confirm, and a change of mode or scope discards an unconfirmed edge. `delegationEdgeRefusal(model, sourceId, targetId)` in `src/shared/delegationGraph.ts` says which drops make no edge at all, and `<DelegationEdgeRefusal>` shows the reason at the drop point: an agent onto itself (reactflow allows it in strict mode), and two `spill` agents, whose edge `buildDelegationGraph` would not keep, so the row would be written and nothing would appear. An edge already drawn opens the same editor, pre-filled, from two places that both call the view's `openEdgeEditor`: its line, through reactflow's `onEdgeClick` (passed only in Edit; with no handler and `selectable: false` reactflow marks the edge `inactive` and it takes no pointer event), and its badge, through `DelegationEdgeData.onOpen` (present only in Edit), which makes the badge a button for a click, Enter and Space. The badge stops its own click: `EdgeLabelRenderer` is a portal, a React event bubbles through a portal to the edge's `<g>`, and the canvas would open the same edge twice. Edges stay `selectable: false` and `focusable: false`: they are controlled with no `onEdgesChange`, so a reactflow selection would never show, and reactflow's edge key handling only selects. The edge under the open editor is raised (`emphasis: 'highlighted'`) so the popover can be matched to its line. See "Writing a relationship from the Delegation Graph" for the writes. Edit is also where an agent a group's graph does not draw is put on it, and the pieces are in `DelegationGraphPlacement.tsx`: `<DelegationAddAgentSelect>` (the toolbar's "Add agent" control, a native select over `sectionAgentsForAdding(agents, groups)`), `<DelegationDropOverlay>`, `<DelegationPlacedNotice>`, and `placedAgentHint(groupName)`, the ONE home of the sentence that says placing is not moving. PLACING IS A LAYOUT GESTURE: the view keeps the placed ids in plain state (`placedIds`, never saved, cleared on a change of scope), hands them to the model, and writes nothing to any agent, least of all its `groupId`. A placed agent is the fourth node state, `placed` (`spill` scope with no edge: dotted like `spill`, full opacity, a "No edge yet" line), and the first edge turns it into an ordinary `spill` node, exactly as if the row had been written in settings; an edge between it and another agent outside the group is refused by `delegationEdgeRefusal()` like any two `spill` agents. Nothing can be placed on a scope with no in-scope agent, because an edge needs one end in scope. An added agent is placed by `arrangeDelegationGraph()` and the view is fitted; a DROPPED one goes where the pointer is and is saved through `saveDroppedPositions()`. The drop zone reads the dragged agent from `uiStore.draggingSessionId`, which is all a Left Bar row sets (it puts nothing in `dataTransfer`), so gate on that field and leave every other drag alone; an agent already drawn is selected and not added. KNOWN GAP: the modal's backdrop covers the Left Bar, so today no Left Bar row can be grabbed while the graph is open and "Add agent" is the reachable path; the drop zone starts working the day the graph can float (`ModalFloatingConfig`). Dragging a node works in both modes, because it changes where an agent is drawn and never whom it can reach, and `deleteKeyCode` is `null` in both: a node is an agent, and an edge is removed through its editor. `useDelegationGraphModel` subscribes to the session store through `delegationRosterEquality()` (`src/renderer/stores/sessionEquality.ts`), which compares exactly the fields `PeerRosterInput` reads, peer rows by VALUE; a plain `useSessionStore((s) => s.sessions)` would rebuild every roster on each batch of streamed output. Persistent layout is the rule, the opposite of the Cue canvas: `arrangeDelegationGraph()` never moves an agent that has a saved position. It returns `{ positions, newlyPlaced }`: with every agent saved, the saved positions; with none saved, the scope's algorithm (the default one for a `manual` scope whose placed agents are all gone); with some saved, each new agent beside the neighbors that have a place, in rounds outward, moved straight down off any agent it landed on, and a new agent with no placed neighbor in a grid under the graph. The canvas hands `newlyPlaced` to `savePlacedPositions()` so the new agent is in the same place on the next open. `computeDelegationLayout()` is what the layout picker calls. It runs the algorithm over the CONNECTED agents only and packs the isolated ones in a grid below, because most agents in a fleet have no peers and an algorithm given all of them draws one row dozens of nodes wide. `hierarchical` and `force` are `applyHierarchicalLayout()` / `applyForceLayout()` from `DocumentGraph/layoutAlgorithms.ts`, which are generic over the node data and take `measureNode` for a node that is not a document card; do not copy them. The force run is seeded on a spiral, so it is deterministic: left at the origin, the simulation starts each node at a random point and two runs differ. `radial` is a radial tree written here (ring = relationships from the center in either direction, a branch inside its lead's wedge, agents with no path on one outer ring); its center is `centerId`, else the most connected agent. Node sizes for all of this come from the leaf `nodes/agentDelegationNodeSize.ts` (`estimateAgentDelegationNodeWidth(name)`), so the layout engine does not import a React component and through it the session store. On the canvas, positions are local state between two saves, and the canvas recognizes the echo of its own save (`savedByCanvasRef`) so a node under the pointer is never pulled back to where it was saved a moment ago; a position change that carries a position (pointer or arrow keys) saves EVERY node. A named layout and a reset ask for confirmation only when the scope is `manual`, since moving between two algorithms destroys nothing the user made. The legend (`DelegationGraphLegend.tsx`) and the panel (`DelegationGraphDetail.tsx`) draw their samples and badges from the tables the canvas uses (`DELEGATION_EDGE_LINES`, `DelegationEdgeBadge`, `AGENT_DELEGATION_NODE_BORDER_STYLE`). The modal is mounted in `AppInfoModals` and is NOT portaled, both on purpose: the Peers link opens the Edit Agent modal on top of the graph, the two share one z-index, and the one later in the DOM paints over the other, so the graph must come first (and rank below `MODAL_PRIORITIES.NEW_INSTANCE`, or Escape would close the graph under the form). It is a `DESTINATION_MODALS` member, so it and the other full-window surfaces replace each other. The view reads its scope once on mount, so the modal keys it by scope: a second `openModal` with another scope shows that scope.
- **Writing a relationship from the Delegation Graph:** `writeDelegationEdge(sourceId, targetId, draft)`, `deleteDelegationEdge(sourceId, targetId)`, `excludeDelegationPeer(sourceId, targetId, description?)`, `describeDelegationEdgeRemoval(removed, sourceName, targetName)`, `DelegationEdgeDraft`, `DelegationEdgeRemoval`, `DELEGATION_EDGE_NEEDS_ACCESS`, `DELEGATION_EDGE_NOT_FOUND` in `src/renderer/services/delegationGraphEdits.ts`; `peerAgentEditsProblem(edits, liveAgentIds, selfId?)`, `resolveGrantedPeer(session, allSessions, peerId)`, `PeerGrantSource`, `SELF_PEER_ERROR`, `DEFAULT_PEER_ACCESS`, `PEER_ACCESS_CONTROLS`, `PEER_NOTE_PLACEHOLDER` in `src/shared/peerAgents.ts`; `<DelegationEdgeEditor>` in `src/renderer/components/DelegationGraph/DelegationEdgeEditor.tsx`. The canvas owns no store of relationships: an edge from A to B IS a row for B in A's `peerAgents`, so drawing one writes to A and to nothing else. B is never written, and the editor never mirrors an edge: when the opposite edge exists it says so in words (`delegationAccessSentence(kind, source, target, { already })` in `edges/DelegationEdge.tsx`) and offers nothing automatic, because a manager who delegates down to an employee who only consults up is two gestures and a third must not undo either. `writeDelegationEdge` makes the write `maestro-cli update-agent --peer` makes: one `PeerAgentEdits` patch (an upsert and a note for the target), checked by `peerAgentEditsProblem` and applied by `applyPeerAgentEdits`, whose result is already through `normalizePeerAgents`. That is what makes a re-drawn direction an EDIT: a second row for one `sessionId` would collapse last-write-wins and silently eat the first. A row a group or worktree grant only derived becomes an explicit one, which then overrides the grant for that one peer; the grant field is not touched. It refuses, with nothing written, an edge with neither bit (not a relationship; the exclusion row is a separate, deliberate act), an agent as its own peer, and an end that is not a live agent. It flushes the agent with `flushAgentToStore()` at once, because the renderer's persistence is debounced and the next turn's `## Peer Agents` block and `show-agent` are built from what main holds. `peerAgentEditsProblem` is the ONE live-agent check: the remote `update-agent` handler (`useAppRemoteEventListeners.ts`, with `selfId`), the group defaults patch (`groupDefaultsPatch.ts`, without one: a seed has no self) and the graph all call it, so do not retype the two error sentences. `PEER_ACCESS_CONTROLS` names the two bits of one peer (`R` / Consult, `W` / Delegate, the square titles and what each does) and `PEER_NOTE_PLACEHOLDER` the note field; the Peers tab rows and the edge editor both read them and both draw the bits with `PermissionSquare`, because the row the editor writes is the row the tab shows. A new row starts as `DEFAULT_PEER_ACCESS` (consult only) on both surfaces. The editor floats at the drop point with `useContextMenuPosition` and registers its own layer (`MODAL_PRIORITIES.DELEGATION_GRAPH_EDGE_EDITOR`, above the graph) so Escape closes it and leaves the graph open; a click elsewhere does NOT close it, because a note typed into it must not be lost to a slipped pointer. TAKING AN EDGE AWAY IS TWO DIFFERENT WRITES, and the editor's delete action (`onDelete`, offered only beside `existing`) calls `deleteDelegationEdge`, which picks between them from the resolved roster: an edge the user wrote has a row, and the row is deleted (`remove`, what `update-agent --remove-peer` sends); an edge a group or worktree grant derived has NO row, so there is nothing to delete, and the peer is excluded with a row that has neither bit (`excludeDelegationPeer`, what `--peer <target>:none` sends), which `resolvePeerRoster` reads as an override of the grant for that one peer. The grant field is never touched. Clearing both squares on an existing edge and saving is the same exclusion and not a delete: the editor allows a draft with neither bit only when `existing` is set (button `Save as excluded`, with a line that says so), and the view routes it to `excludeDelegationPeer` with the note as typed. The results leave different things in the agent's settings, so each is said at the moment it happens: the button reads `Delete edge` for a row and `Exclude peer` for a derived edge, and the view raises ONE toast from `describeDelegationEdgeRemoval`, the single home of that copy (row removed; row removed but a grant still draws the edge, `restoredBy`; excluded over a grant, `overrides`; excluded with no grant under it). Never present an exclusion as a plain delete: a user who then switches the grant off is left with a row they did not know they wrote, so the toast names the Peers tab as the place to remove it. `resolveGrantedPeer` answers "what is under this row" (the roster with the explicit rows set aside); it is `resolvePeerRoster` with one field cleared, so do not restate precedence to find out which grant covers a peer. A pair with no edge is refused (`DELEGATION_EDGE_NOT_FOUND`) with nothing written. A plain save raises no toast.
- **Running a consult (prompt, budgets, completion, Stop):** `createConsultService()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/agents/consult.ts` is the one place a cross-agent consult is dispatched; `start(request, opts)` builds the prompt (`buildCrossAgentPrompt` in `agents/consult-prompt.ts`), launches the target as an ephemeral `cross-agent-<requestId>` process through a `ConsultRunner`, and settles with exactly ONE terminal chunk. `cancelForSource(sourceSessionId)` is Stop.
